2100. Provisions of the Act 

2110. Appropriations 2120. 

The first section of each of the public assistance titles of the Social Security Act, as amended, authorizes an appropriation for each fiscal year of "a sum sufficient to carry cut the purposes" of each of the titles, and states: "The sums made available under this section shall be used for making payments to States which have submitted, and had approved by the Secretary of Health, Education, and Welfare. 

State plans for old-age assistance" aid to families with dependent children; aid to the blind; aid to the permanently and totally disabled; aid to the aged, blind or disabled]. 

(Title I, sec. 1; title IV, sec. 401; title X, sec. 1001; 

title XIV, sec. 1401; and title XVI, sec 1601.

) 

Payments to States 

Sections 3, 403, 1003, 1403, and 1603 provide that from the sums appropriated therefor, the Secretary of the Treasury shall pay to each State which has an approved plan an amount based on the amounts expended by the State under the plan as assistance in the form of money payments and medical or any other type of remedial care, and costs of administration, including services to individuals. 

Formulas.--The formulas for determining the Federal share are included in subsection (a) of sections 3, 403, 1003, 1403, and 1603. complete text of these sections is in Ilandbook I-1000. "(1) The Secretary of Health, Education, and Welfare shall, prior to the beginning of each quarter, estimate the amount

Interpretation 

Payments to States  

3/19/63 

Federal Appropriation 

An authorization for annual appropriations for Federal payments to States for public assistance is contained in each of the public assistance titles of the Social Security Act. The authorization is for "a sum sufficient to carry out the purposes" of each of the titles. This method differs from most Federal grants-in-aid, which have a fixed-sum appropriation, within which the allocations to States are made. For public assistance, the amount of the Federal payment is determined by applying the formulas specified in the act to the amount of the State's expenditures under the State plan for assistance and administration; it is not subject to an over-all limit on the amount that a State may receive (except for Puerto Rico, the Virgin Islands and Guam). This type of financing is often referred to as "open-end appropriation. 

Method of Making Payments to States 

The public assistance grant-in-aid system under the Social Security Act is designed to provide regular and continuous financial support to State programs, so that the States, in turn, can assure promptness and regularity in making payments to needy eligible individuals. To achieve this, the Act provides for advances of funds to States prior to the beginning of each quarter, based on the estimated needs of the States and subject to later adjustment based on actual expenditures. Federal estimates of the amounts to be paid to States are based on the quarterly estimates by the States of the amounts to be expended, the State funds certified by the State as available, and such other facts and investigation as may be necessary. These estimates are increased or decreased by under- and over-payments for prior periods determined on the basis of reports of actual expenditures.

Grants for the quarter are approved by the Commissioner of Welfare before payments are made. Immediately following the approval by the 

Commissioner of a quarterly grant, the State agency is notified of the action. This approval constitutes a certification that the State plan continues to meet the requirements of the Social Security Act. 

Transmitted by State Letter No. 636

p.2 

Part V 

2000-2999 

 Payments to States  

3/19/63 2220. 

Methods of Making Payments to States (Continued) 

2230. 2231. 

Approval by the Commissioner and certification for payment are made to the United States Treasury Department prior to the beginning of a quarter unless, for example, Federal funds or State funds are unavailable, or there are unresolved questions of a State's conformity with the Social Security Act, or there is a delay in the State's submittal of necessary fiscal reports.

One important provision in the public assistance appropriation acts helps to carry out the objective of uninterrupted payments to States. 

This provision permits the approval and certification of payments to States, after May 31, for the first quarter of the next fiscal year, when the Federal appropriation for that year has not been enacted by that date. This provision is invoked to make possible the certification to the United States Treasury Department for payment, prior to the beginning of that quarter, in accordance with the provisions of the Social 

Security Act. 

Payments to States are made monthly by the United States Treasury Department; payment is in the form of a single check to the State for all programs insofar as possible.

Formulas for Determining Amount of Federal Payment 

The basis for determining the amount to be paid to States is described in the Social Security Act. The amount is based on the State's (1) expenditures for assistance and (2) costs of administration (see V-2120).

For a tabular presentation of the formulas currently in effect, see chart which follows on "Legislative Provisions for Determining Federal

Share of State Expenditures for Assistance Payments and for Adminis tration, Services, and Training."

Assistance (Other Than Medical Assistance for the Aged) 

The basic formula for determining the Federal share of assistance is in two parts. Effective October 1, 1962, in the first part, the Federal

Transmitted by State Letter No. 636

p.2 

Part V 

2000-2999 

  Payments to States 

3/19/63 

2231. Assistance (Other Than Medical Assistance for the Aged) (Continued) 

share is 29/35 of the first $35 of the average monthly payment per recipient for the aged, blind, and disabled under titles I, X, and XIV, or per recipient of aid to the aged, blind, or disabled under title XVI; and 

14/17 of the first $17 per recipient in aid to families with dependent children.

The second part of the formula is applicable to monthly expenditures in excess of the amounts stated above, up to the prescribed limit of $70 times the number of (1) recipients of old age assistance, aid to the blind, and aid to the permanently and totally disabled, or (2) recipients of aid to the aged, blind, or disabled (under title XVI); and $30 times the number of recipients for aid to families with dependent children. 

The Federal percentage to be applied for this part of the formula is derived by relating the State's per capita income to the national per capita income. This percentage will range among States from a minimum of 50 percent to a maximum of 65 percent. 

The Federal percentage is "100 per centum less the State percentage"; and the State percentage "bears the same ratio to 50 per centum as the square of the per capita income of such State bears to the square of the per capita income of the United States." 

The "Federal percentage" to be applied for this part of the formula is promulgated by the Secretary of Health, Education, and Welfare at stated intervals as described in Handbook V-2233, which follows. A copy of the current promulgation is included therein. 

2231,1 Vendor Medical Payments 

Titles I and XVI 

In addition to the basic formula, as described above, for determining the Federal share of old-age assistance under title I and aid to the aged, blind or disabled under title XVI, there is additional Federal financial participation provided to States that make vendor medical payments. 

Two alternative formulas have been provided to determine the additional Federal funds for such vendor medical payments. The formula to be 

Transmitted by State Letter No. 636

2231.p.2 

2000-2999 

 Payments to States 

3/19/63 

2231.1 Vendor Medical Payments 

Titles I and XVI (Continued) 

applied on and after October 1, 1962, for any individual State in a specified quarter is the one of two alternatives that yields the larger amount of additional Federal funds for that State in that quarter: (1) An amount equal to 15% of vendor medical payments each month up to a maximum of $15 per recipient per month from Federal, State and local funds; or (2) An amount equal to the "Federal medical percentage" of the amount of vendor medical payments up to a maximum of $15 per recipient per month, or of the amount by which the total average payment exceeds $70, whichever is less. 

Thus, additional Federal participation in vendor medical payments cannot be earned in more than a total of $15 per recipient per month. 

The "Federal medical percentage" differs from the "Federal percentage" used in the basic formula for determining the Federal share of old-age assistance, in that the former varies from a minimum of 50% to a maximum of 80%, whereas the latter varies from a minimum of 50% to a maximum of 65%. In both instances, the percentage is based upon the relationship of the State's per capita income to the national per capita income. A copy of the current promulgation of both is included in 

Handbook V-2233. 

2231.11 Medical Assistance for the Aged - Vendor Medical Payments Titles I and XVI 

The formula for determining the Federal share of assistance expenditures for medical assistance for the aged is the "Federal medical percentage" of the State's monthly expenditures, with no maximum for any jurisdiction (see Handbook Supplement - MAA, M-8231).

2231.21 Federal Participation in Assistance and Administration 

The law provides specifically that the total amount of Federal funds certified by the Secretary for participation in payments of assistance and administration under plans approved under titles I (except assistance under Medical Assistance for the Aged), IV, X, XIV, and XVI (except assistance under Medical Assistance for the Aged) shall not exceed for any fiscal year ending after June 30, 1962: $9, 800, 000 for 

Puerto Rico; $330,000 for the Virgin Islands and $450, 000 for Guam. 

Of such sums, specified amounts (as set forth in V-2231.22) may be used only for additional Federal participation in vendor medical payments.

The Federal share of expenditures of assistance is 50% of such monthly expenditures up to $37.50 times the number of (1) recipients of old-age assistance, aid to the blind, and aid to the permanently and totally disabled, or (2) recipients of aid to the aged, blind, or disabled (under title XVI); and up to $18 times the number of recipients of aid to families with dependent children. There is no statutory limit on the amount of assistance expenditures made for medical assistance for the aged under title I or XVI. Federal participation in such expenditures is available at 50%.

The Federal share of expenditures of administration, except for the Federal share in the costs of providing certain social services (see 

IV-4800) and for training (see III-3400), is 50% of such expenditures as are found by the Secretary to be necessary for the proper administration of the public assistance titles of the Social Security Act. 

2231.22 Additional Federal Participation in Vendor Medical Payments 

Additional Federal participation is also available in vendor medical payments made under title I or XVI (both excluding payments as medical assistance for the aged) not to exceed for any fiscal year ending after June 30, 1962, $625,000 for Puerto Rico; $18,750 for the Virgin Islands and $25,000 for Guam. The additional Federal participation will be the larger of the following two alternative

2231.3 Navajo and Hopi Indians 2232.

A special provision (Section 9 of Public Law 474, 81st Congress) makes available additional Federal participation of 80% of the State's regular share of assistance payments (excluding medical assistance for the aged) to certain needy Navajo and Hopi Indians, within the maxima specified under titles I, IV, and X. (Only three States--Utah, Arizona and New 

Mexico--are affected.)

B. Administration, Services, and Training 

Effective 9/1/62 I. All programs.--75 percent of total sums expended for (1) training staff of 

State and local agencies and (2) costs of rehabilitative and other services (to persons in financial need or likely to become so) as specified by the Secretary of Health, Education, and Welfare, for purposes under each pro gram as follows: 

Old-age assistance and medical assistance for the aged: 

To attain or retain capability for self-care. 

Aid to the aged, blind, or disabled: 

Aid to the blind:

Aid to the permanently and totally disabled: 

To attain or retain capability for self-support or self-care. 

Aid to families with dependent children:

To help maintain and strengthen family life and to help children's parents or relatives attain or retain capability for maximum selfsupport and personal independence consistent with the maintenance of continuing parental care and protection.

II. All programs.--One-half of the total costs of all services and administrative expenses, other than those in I above, necessary for the proper and efficient administration of the State plan. Effective 7/1/63 All programs.--If a State provides a minimum of services prescribed by the 

Secretary of Health, Education, and Welfare for a program, the provisions described above under effective date of 9/1/62 apply. Otherwise, the Federal share of the cost of all administration, services, and training for that program is one-half. 1

2240. Action Under Other Laws Affecting Public Assistance Payments 

"Hatch Act" Violation.--In the enforcement of the Hatch Act (Act of 

August 12, 1939, as amended), when the United States Civil Service Commission finds that an officer or employee of a Stare agency, subject to the provisions of such act, has committed a violation thereof under the conditions set forth in Section 12 of the Hatch Act, "The Commissioner shall make and certify to the appropriate Federal agency an Order requiring it to withhold from its loans or grants to the State or local agency to which such notification was given, an amount equal to two years' compensation at the rate such officer or employee was receiving at the time of such violation. 11 

Delinquent Payments for OASDI Purposes.--Where a State is delinquent with respect to contributions, including interest thereon, for UASDI purposes under its Section 218 agreement with the Secretary, pursuant to the authority under Section 218 (3) of Title II of the Social. 

Security Act, the Commissioner of Social Security may, in approving a quarterly grant to the State for one of the categorical programs, direct deduction, from the quarterly grant, of the delinquent OASDI amounts to be credited by the United States Treasury Department to the 

CASDI Trust Fund Account. 

2250. Initial Payment to States

Payments to a State begin when a plan for an assistance program under any of the four public assistance titles has been approved. They continue to be made as long as the State's plan and operation under the plan continue to be in compliance with the requirements of the Social Security Act. The first payment. to a State under an approved plan may be made with respect to the quarter in which such plan was submitted for approval, but may not be made with respect to any prior quarter. Such payment may include the Federal share of administrative cosis incurred in the preparation of a State plan.

2300. The Payment Process 

The forms and the instructions for their use are included in 

Handbook VII-5100-5200. 

2310. Submittal of States' Quarterly Reports of Estimated Expenditures and Supporting Documents (Forms PA-155ABD, 155C, 155.1, 

Estimates of funds to be expended for assistance and administration must be submitted quarterly for each category with supporting documents: (1) a statement in justification of the amounts estimated, explaining any anticipated increase or decrease in caseload or average payment and in administrative costs for the program, over those of the past quarter, and (2) a certification showing that there are sufficient

State funds available to cover the estimated amount of State funds subject to Federal participation, as well as the amount not subject to Federal participation. A State's estimates must be submitted on the prescribed forms, six weeks prior to the beginning of the estimate quarter. 

2311. Preparation of Estimates 

In computing their estimates, States must take into consideration the number of persons expected to receive assistance under each category during each month of the quarter, the estimated average monthly assistance payments, and total estimated expenditures for assistance and administration. Estimated expenditures for assistance payments in excess of the average maximums and other payments not computable for 

The State and

Federal funds must be deducted from the total estimate. 

Federal shares are then computed in accordance with the formulas prescribed in the Social Security Act. These reports must also show that 

State and local funds appropriated and expected to be available for assistance and administration during the estimate quarter will be sufficient for the State's share of the estimated expenditures. 

Because of the large aggregate amount of Federal funds involved in State programs, States must anticipate actual expenditures as realistically as possible and test them against the trends in program caseload, average payment, and cost of administration during the current quarter. 

The report of estimated expenditures must be signed by the executive officer of the State welfare agency or his designated representative. 

Signing the estimates is a certification by the State official that the information contained in the forms has been carefully prepared and presents as realistic an estimate of the anticipated expenditures as possible. 

Transmitted by State Letter No. 355

P. 2 

2000-2999 

 Payments to States 

12/1/58 

2311. Preparation of Estimates (Continued) 

2312.

The estimates are submitted to the regional office of the Department of Health, Education, and Welfare. The submittal letter must be signed by a State director or a person officially designated by him to act in his absence; in signing the letter, the State official, in effect, certifies that the State plan meets all requirements of the Social Security Act, and that all changes in the State's plan have been submitted through the regular plan submittal procedures. 

Navajo and Hopi Indians.For States affected by Section 9, Public 

Law 474, 81st Congress, relating to certain Navajo and Hopi Indians, additional estimate forms are prescribed.

Supplemental or Revised Estimates 

If, after submitting quarterly estimates on the basis of which grants have been approved, a State finds that additional funds are needed urgently, it may submit, revised quarterly estimates with explanation of changes, including the increased monthly caseload, average payments, or both, as needed to provide the basis for computing the amount cf additional funds to be included in a supplemental grant. 

2320. States Quarterly Reports of Expenditures (Forms PA-142, 142.1 

through 12.9)

States must account for Federal funds by submitting statements of expenditures within 30 days after the close of each quarter. These statements, accepted subject to Federal audit, serve as a claim on the Federal Government, and a statement of accountability for Federal funds advanced for a specific purpose. 

The expenditure report for the second preceding quarter is used in computing the net adjustment to be applied to the payments for the quarter under consideration.

A State's quarterly reports of expenditures must be submitted on the prescribed forms. The reports must be signed by the executive officer of the State welfare agency or his designated representative and mailed directly to the Washington office.

Navajo and Hopi Indians.State agencies affected by Section 9, Public 

Law 474, 81st Congress, are to report on supplemental forms prescribed for reporting assistance to Navajo and Hopi Indians.

2330. Federal Review, Certification and Payment of Grants 2331. Review of States' Quarterly Estimates by Regional Public Assistance 

Staff

The public assistance regional representative reviews the estimate and supporting materials as to the propriety of the estimate, availability of State funds and the adequacy of the justification statement; confers with State officials regarding them, if necessary; and makes such reports and recommendations to the central office as are appropriate concerning any questions of continuing compliance with the requirements of the Social Security Act, as amended, in plan or practice, and concerning action on the State's estimate. 

2332. Review in Washington Office

Staff in the central office of the Bureau review the reports of estimated expenditures for validity, completeness and mathematical accuracy, in conjunction with the recommendations made by the public assistance regional representative as to amount to be granted and the continuing compliance of the State programs with the requirements of the Social 

Security Act, as amended.

A State's estimate is questioned if in the light of the State's justification statement and all other information available, it appears to be unrealistic in relation to the amount that will be used in the estimate quarter. In such a case a State is asked to submit additional information in justification or to change its estimate. 

Expenditure reports and supporting schedules are reviewed for completeness, proper reporting, and mathematical accuracy. The balance of Federal funds to be accounted for (or Federal funds due the State) is checked and reconciled with the operating accounts maintained in the Bureau of Public Assistance. (A post-audit of Federal accounts is made by the 

United States General Accounting Office, using the State's report of estimated expenditures, the State's report of expenditures for the second preceding quarter, and Computation Sheet (PA-110 revised)). 

2333. Computation of Payment 

Grants are divided into three equal parts for monthly payment and the adjustments made against the amount which would otherwise be due for the first month of the quarter. The amount of Federal funds to be paid

to a State is computed by applying the pending adjustments against the estimates for the estimate quarter. The net adjustment is derived by taking the difference between the Federal estimate and total actual Federal participation in current disbursements as reported by the State for the same quarter, net adjustments for prior periods, allowances or disallowances by Federal audit, collections, and other additions or deductions from Federal funds held by the State as reported. 

If it works a hardship on the State in meeting its current financial obligations to accept all adjustments in the payment for the first month of the quarter, the Commissioner of Social Security will, upon written request of the State, consider prorating the adjustments over a longer period.

Fiscal Accountability of State Agencies 

Basic principles of proper and efficient operation of any public welfare activity are: (1) that there be a specific location of responsibility for auministering the basic law, which authorizes the activity, with funds provided under authority of an appropriation: (2) that the agency currently observe and discharge its accountability for the use of all funds in accordance with the administrative

Part V. 

3000-3999 

8/16/49 

Fiscal Accountability of State Agencies (Continued) 

and fiscal controls established by its budget system and by the basic law, appropriation acts, and other related laws; and (3) that the administrator of the agency report the agency's discharge of proper accountability for expenditures from funds, made available during each governmental fiscal period, after the agency has settled its accounts for that period. 

The assistance titles of the Social Security Act establish a system of Federal grants-in-aid on a quarterly basis with payments released monthly to States with approved State plans, under conditions and limitations specified by the provisions of these titles. Under the Social Security Act the Administrator of the 

Federal Security Agency is held accountable for the use of Federal funds appropriated for this purpose (a) in accordance with fiscal controls over the amount and the payment of grants to States, established by the Social Security Act, and (b) in accordance with fiscal controls maintained over all Federal agencies' expenditures.

Responsibility is delegated to the Bureau of Public Assistance through the Social Security Administration (a) to interpret Federal requirements and to assist the States to make more effective use of Federal aid, and (b) to assure itself that claims made by the State with regard to expenditures are proper. Ultimate settlement and adjustment of accounts are the responsibility of the General Accounting Office, and no Federal agency or official can limit the right of the Federal Government to recover funds due it. 

The analysis and approval of State plans, the fiscal audit, the administrative review, and the personnel review are the principarmeans established by the Social Security Administration for the observance of its accountability under titles I, IV, and X of the In the observance and discharge of the Federal agency's accountability, the proper and efficient operation of each State plan within the established framework of the State government is an important factor. act.

As a State agency operating a State program, the public assistance agency is responsible for maintaining such methods of administration as will assure the discharge of its accountability for all funds from all sources, and will assure a proper reporting of its accountability during and after the completion of each State fiscal period. 

Because Federal grants are based on a proportionate share of the sum of all expenditures under the plan which are subject to Federal financial participation, the State agency is responsible for reporting to the Federal agency all expenditures under the State plan. The requirements for reporting all expenditures on a quarterly basis to the Federal agency are not meant to distort or interfere with the 

State's own pattern of reporting.

Part V. 

3000-3999 

  

Accountability for Federal Funds Advanced 8/16/19 

Fiscal Accountability of State Agencies (Continued) 

An organized and supervised system of records is needed by the State agency to supply evidence to support State claims and to furnish data for required reports. These records can be reliable and comparable only as the State agency's rules, regulations, administrative procedures, and supervision are adequate and available to all concerned in order to insure the necessary degree of uniformity in record-keeping and report-making throughout the State. It is the responsibility of the State agency to see that all the expenditures for which Federal participation is claimed are properly supported by written evidence, conveniently accessible for examination, that payments were made in proper amounts to eligible persons, and that goods were received and services were rendered in accordance with all requirements governing the expenditures involved. Adequate supervision of local administrative units is essential in carrying out this responsibility. Such supervision should include periodic audits. The responsibility of the 

State public assistance agency for audit of the expenditures made under its State plan and for reporting adjustments arising from such audits is not modified in any degree by the fact of a Federal audit. This also holds true with respect to the Federal administrative review, whether or not the information is secured solely by the staff of the Bureau of Public Assistance or is based in part on information secured by the State agency through its own activities. 

Federal reviews of State agency operations are not substitutes for a State agency's own activities in supervision and control. 

Federal approval of a State plan and the availability of funds for the State's share of the cost of assistance and administration under the plan establish the basis for continuing quarterly grants to a State and a continuing State-Federal relationship. In this relationship, the State agency is expected to operate in accordance with the plan, as approved, and to include in its claim for Federal funds only those expenditures for assistance and for administration which are properly subject to Federal participation and only those portions of such assistance payments as are within the Federal maximums. The 

State agency is likewise responsible for providing information through State plan material, through quarterly estimates and quarterly statements of expenditures, and through other required reports and communications to enable the Commissioner for Social Security to make proper determination of the amount of each quarterly advance of Federal funds to the State for each category of assistance.

3210 

Part V. 

3000-3999 3210. 3220. 

 Accountability for Federal Funds Advanced 

8/16/49 

Meaning of State Agency Fiscal Accountability.--Fiscal account ability refers to the agency's obligation to render an accounting for the use of funds provided for activities under the approved 

State plan and includes responsibility for: 

(a) maintaining an adequate accounting system, adapted to the nature of an assistance program and to such special factors as whether the program is Stateadministered or State-supervised, whether it is financed from Federal and State or from Federal,

State, and local funds, whether there is centralized or local disbursement, etc.; (b) maintaining adequate systems of case records and of statistical reporting; and (c) preparing from accounting records and submitting to the Social Security Administration dependable summary fiscal reports accounting for expenditures made under the State plan and properly subject to State claims for Federal financial participation.

Nature and Scope of State Agency Accountability.--Under the pro visions of the Federal act, each quarterly advance of Federal funds reflects adjustments in amounts reported as State claims for prior periods. The system of quarterly grants necessitates a reporting of expenditures for each completed quarterly period. 

The Commissioner for Social Security is required to make adjustments in advances to States for any prior quarterly period in which it is found that the State's claim was erroneous. To avoid the possibility of large accumulated adjustments in some future period the State agency should report adjustments for any prior period at any time it discovers errors in claims for such periods. 

In rare instances, a State agency may discover fraudulent practices of many years standing. Or, in the course of investigation into the administration of a particular phase of the program or into the administration of the program by a particular local unit, the State agency may uncover practices which would not warrant claim for Federal financial participation in the expenditures concerned. 

In such instances, the State agency is responsible for reporting adjustments for the quarterly periods in which such expenditures were made. The Social Security Act specifies that whenever recoveries or collections are made with respect to expenditures in which Federal financial participation has been claimed it is the responsibility of the State agency to report adjustments promptly for the Federal share of such recoveries and collections. The observance of strict State agency accountability is the best protection of the State against loss in a current year of anticipated amounts of Federal grants by reason of adjustments in claims for long past quarterly periods.

Part V. 

3000-3999 

  

Accountability for Federal Funds Advanced 8/16/49

Nature and Scope of State Agency Accountability (Continued) 

The scope of State agency accountability is not limited to expenditures from funds under the immediate control of the State agency office. It extends to all expenditures made under the State plan in which Federal participation is claimed. 

The State agency's accountability is the same regardless of the source of the funds and regardless of what unit of government makes the disbursement and maintains the basic accounting record of the transaction.

Under the Social Security Act, the responsibility of the State agency is primary. As a means of assuring the observance and discharge of its fiscal accountability to the Federal agency, the State agency is responsible for assuring itself, through proper staff instruction and the normal administrative processes of supervision and control, that expenditures included in the computation of its claims for Federal funds are proper and are supported by adequate and dependable records.

An "essential person" or other ineligible person who is living with the eligible person may not be counted as a recipient.

Records in Support of Count 

The major records which support the recipient count are (1) the basic authorizations of award, and (2) the monthly assistance rolls which represent a record of new, continued, and revised payments to recipients. Awards may be authorized separately for each recipient or one authorization may cover more than one recipient in the same family unit. Similarly, assistance rolls may list each eligible recipient separately by name and amount of award or show the names of two or more recipients who are to receive certain specified portions of an aggregate payment. The accuracy of the recipient count would be affected if any joint or group actions did not establish the status of a "recipient" or "child" as a separate and identifiable recipient of the assistance. 1. 

Authorization of Award 

It is a basic requirement that all payments be supported by an authorization of award signed by the official or officials charged with such responsibility for each recipient who is to be counted in the State's claim: a. 

Single authorizations 

Authorizations of award for recipients of QAA, AB, APTD or AABD must indicate the name of the recipient and the amount of the award. 

An authorization of award for AFDC must indicate the name of the grantee-relative or emergency payee, the name of the needy relative or relatives (grantee or other) included in the group to whom the Federal matching formula is to be applied, the number and the dates of birth of the eligible children, and the amount of the award. This requirement does not mean that the completion of the authorization of award need be delayed pending the establishment of exact

2. 8. dates of birth of children obviously under the age limit. 

In such cases it is satisfactory to enter the approximate age or year of birth until the exact date of birth is established. (See IV-3240.) 

Joint authorizations

An authorization of award which covers more than one recipient, in any program or combination of programs, must show the name of each recipient and the amount of the award attributable to each from each category, except that in an AFDC authorization of award the name of the grantee, the name of the needy relative or relatives (grantee or other) included in the group to whom the Federal matching formula is to be applied, the number and the dates of birth of the eligible children and the aggregate award are sufficient, and amounts attributable to each eligible child need not be shown.

Assistance Rolls 

The periodic complete listing of recipients, in the form of an assistance roll or other acceptable type of listing device, which is used as the basis for payment, must reflect accurately the charges to each category and the amount of award attributable to individual recipients, or payees in the AFDC program. Therefore, the following information is required to be shown on assistance rolls: a.

The name of each payee for the assistance grant under QAA, AB, APTD, or AABD and the name of the eligible recipient where the payee is not the recipient. For AFDC only the payee's name need be listed and the number of eligible recipients, i.e., the number of children and the needy relative or relatives who are included as eligible recipients for the purposes of the Federal matching formula computation.

The program category under which the awards were made.

Safeguarding Accuracy of Count 

The following safeguards are considered necessary to insure the accuracy of the States' computations: 

1. An "assistance roll" or other complete listing of recipients covering each group disbursement for a month, or other regular period, must be available for examination; 2. 3. 4.

A written procedure for making the count of recipients which is applicable throughout the State, must be provided which is reasonably calculated to insure uniformly accurate results;

Provision must be made for verification of the correctness of State and local agency counts, including the adequate supervision of the personnel involved and periodic review of the efficiency of the procedures themselves; and

A record of the computation of the number of recipients must be maintained in the central office of the State agency, which is adequate for purposes of audit.

3/5/51 3340. Recoveries.-For the purpose of reporting to the Federal Government, a recovery may be defined as any recoupment by the State or its political subdivisions of part or all of one or more assistance payments which have been included in amounts claimed for Federal participation.

The Social Security Act does not require that States make recoveries of amounts of assistance paid to recipients. However, where a State plan provides for recoveries of assistance payments, a share of the net recoveries proportionate to the share of assistance paid to the individual from Federal funds under the approved State plan must be paid to the United States Government. In determining any net recovery there is to be deducted the actual cost incurred in making the recovery insofar as it constitutes a specific lien or offset against the fund recovered. In determining the State's accountability to the United States by reason of net recoveries of assistance the guiding principle is that the rights of the United States are of equal rank with those of the State in respect of all items which were taken into account originally in the Federal grant or in respect of which the Federal Government participated. As respects the ranking of claims against an estate, generally, the schedule of priorities as provided by State law, such for example as in the decedent's estate law of any State, is appropriately considered as part of the plan.

In accounting to the Federal Government a recovery should be classified as (1) a refund if it relates to an overpayment of assistance for particular months or (2) a collection if the recovery is not related to overpayment for specific months but is of a general nature resulting from the right of the State to recover from or on behalf of the recipient or his estate all assistance paid to him. 

The methods of computing and reporting the Federal share of refunds and collections are discussed below. 

3341. Procedure for Reporting Refunds.--Cash_refunds obtained from recipients because of overpayment for particular months should not be reported with "Federal share of collections received" (in Item 3 

of Form PA-142, Quarterly Statement of Expenditures). If the State subsequently determines that the payment for a previous month or months was too large or that the recipient was not entitled to any amount, the refund constitutes an adjustment of the award and should be reported as a decrease in expenditures in the appropriate item of Schedule A, or Schedule B, in accordance with the instructions for those items.

3342 

  

Accountability for Federal Funds Advanced 3/5/51 

Part V. 

3000-3999 

3342. Procedure for Reporting Collections--In the original Social Security 

Act specific provision with respect to the State's accountability for collections was made only with respect to old-age assistance; and 

Section 2(a) (7) thereof provided for the States to make direct remittances of the proper share of such collections to the Federal Government.

The amendments of the act approved August 10, 1939, and August 28, 1950, make specific provision with respect to the State's accountability for collections in all four public assistance programs, and provide for a different method of crediting the Federal Government with its share of such recoveries. Titles I, IV, and X, commencing 

January 1, 1940 and title XIV, commencing October 1, 1950 7provide that certification of grants to the Secretary of the Treasury shall be "*** reduced by a sum equivalent to the pro rata share to which the United States is equitably entitled, as determined by the Administrator, of the net amount recovered during any prior quarter by the State or any political subdivision thereof with respect to old-age assistance or aid to the blind or aid to dependent_children or aid to the permanently and totally 

It will be noted that under this revision of the act, remittance of collections to the Federal Government are no longer necessary, since the law now contemplates that grants to the States shall be reduced. by the amount of the collections to which the United States is equitably entitled. Therefore, States are requested to report the 

Federal portion of collections under item 3 of the Quarterly Statement of Expenditures, Form PA-142, with the understanding that the amounts so reported will be deducted from the grants for an ensuing quarter. 

3343. Rule Governing Collections--The following rule with respect to collections made under the categories old-age assistance, aid to the blind, aid to dependent children (effective August 10, 1940) and aid to the permanently and totally disabled (effective October 1, 1950, is to be applied to the total period for which the collection is made:

Whenever, after making any assistance payments under an approved plan to any aged, blind, or permanently and totally disabled individual or with respect to any dependent child, the State receives any sum of money as total or partial reimbursement for 1

3343+ 

. 

3000-3999 

Accountability for Federal Funds Advanced 3/5/51 

Rules Governing Collections 

such assistance payments, the State shall report the Federal share of the net amount of such collections on Form PA-142 in accordance with the instructions regarding item 3 of this form NII-52007 regardless of the source of such collections, whether repaid by the individual recipient or by his estate, or by some third person on account of such assistance, or whether representing the proceeds of property transferred by such recipient, his estate, or any such third person. The Federal share, which shall be deducted from sums subsequently certifield for payments to the State under the appropriate title of the Social Security Act, shall constitute the same proportion of such collections as the total Federal participation in the assistance payments (forming the basis of such collection) bears to the total assistance paid such individual. 

The assistance payments forming the basis for a collection represent the amount which the State sought to recover under the provisions of its laws, from, or on behalf of, the recipient or his estate. It is the total amount of the legal claim on which the collection is based. Both matchable and non-matchable payments may be included in the claim. Assistance payments made prior to the beginning of Federal participation in the specific case are allowable as part of the basis for the collection and as prior claims against funds collected provided that (a) such assistance is recoverable under statute or common law of the State; (b) the collection did not arise from the operation of lien laws which restrict the benefit of the lien to expenditures for categorical assistance in which the Federal Government participated; and (c) such assistance was included in the amount sought to be collected. Supplementary assistance payments not matched by Federal funds but made during the period in which regular assistance payments were matched may not be deducted from the amount recovered but may be considered in computing the State's share in net recoveries. 

3344. Procedure for Computing the Federal Share of Collections 

The computation of the Federal share of collections from estates of recipients is to be made as follows:

Part V 

3000-3999 

  

Accountability for Federal Funds Advanced 3/5/51 

Procedure for Computing the Federal Share of Collections (continued) Deductions from Gross Collections 

(1) Deductions should first be made from gross collections of the actual cost of collection, insofar as reasonable, such as filing or recording fees, court costs, and outside attorney fees. There should also be deducted expenses reasonably incurred in protecting, as well as in realizing upon, any security or other property assigned to or held by the State out of which such recovery may have been effected.

Salaries and travel of agency employees and expenses of operating agency collection departments or units cannot be deducted from gross collections. Such costs are subject to Federal participation as administrative expenses of the agency after allocation under the agency's approved cost allocation plan. (2) Deductions should then be made of payments made by the State (or by a political subdivision of the State) under statutory priorities--such as funeral expenses and expenses of last illness which are accorded preferred status under most State laws. Even in the absence of a statutory priority, deductions for funeral expenses paid by the State (or by a political subdivision) from collections made from the estates of old-age assistance, aid to the blind and aid to the permanently and totally disabled recipients are proper since the Social Security Amendments of 1939 and 1950 provide with respect to titles I, X, and XIV: "That any part of the amount recovered from the estate of a deceased recipient which is not in excess of the amount expended by the State or any political subdivision thereof for the funeral expenses of the deceased shall not be considered as a basis for reduction under clause (B) of this paragraph."

Funeral expenses paid by State or local agencies which are reimbursed from other sources, (for example, from funds made available under Title II of the Social Security Act) are not deductible from gross collections.

Optional Simplified Methods To Use in Computing Federal Share of Collections State agencies may use a simplified method for computing the Federal share of collections. States wishing to apply simplified methods for computing the Federal share of collections shall submit plan material, for prior approval, showing: (1) the methods to be used (as illustrated by the methods described below); (2) supporting data demonstrating that on the basis of experience with case-by-case computations the application of the proposed method would result in a reasonable determination of the Federal share of collections that adequately protects the Federal interest; and (3) that the method will be consistently applied to all collections in the program in which it is used. 

A method which has been approved may continue in effect until the Federal Bureau or the State agency believes that it no longer produces an equitable distribution of the collections. 

In submitting a substitute proposal, States shall submit plan material for prior approval, as outlined above. 

The State may establish periodically for each public assistance program a flat percentage to be applied to all collections, which is derived from such methods as are illustrated below, or some other equitable method that the State may develop. 1. 

Illustrative Methods 

(a) Case-by-case computation of a sample of collections received.--Under this method the State selects a representative sample of collections received during the year and computes the Federal share of these collections on a case-by-case basis, using the formula described above in c under "Case-by-Case Basis." The sample must be selected on an unbiased basis. The percentage of the Federal share of collections, as determined by the sample, is then applied to the total collections received during the year in determining the Federal share. 

Transmitted by State Letter No. 243

Part V. 

3000-3999 

5/6/55 

Procedure for Computing the Federal Share of Collections (continued) 

Among methods that may be considered by the agency in determining how the sample will be selected are the following: (1) Spreading the sample over the fiscal year.-- 

Under this method the agency selects a sample of the individual collections received each month and computes the Federal share of these selected collections on the case-by-case method. At the end of the year the total amount and the Federal share of the collections sampled for the year are determined. The per- centage of the Federal share of the sample for the year becomes the basis for distributing total collections for that year. Not less than 

10% of the total number of collections received each year should be selected as the sample. То assure unbiased sampling, the sample might be selected according to predetermined case number endings. For example, an examination of collections received each month for cases with case numbers ending in 6 would probably result in an examination of about 10% of all collections. 

If the total number of collections is small and a 10% sample is not sufficient to provide a satisfactory basis for distributing the total collections for the fiscal year, a sample of 

20% or 30% may be necessary. (For example, an examination of collections for cases with case numbers ending in 1, 4, and 9 would probably result in an examination of about 30% of all collections.) 

Since the percentage representing the Federal share of collections derived from the sample is not known until the end of the year, the agency may use an estimated percentage for computing the Federal share currently. This could be the percentage of the Federal share of collections reported during the previous year. At the end of the current year, the agency adjusts for the difference between the estimated percentage used and the actual percentage derived from the current year's sample.

Transmitted by State Letter No. 243

. 

3000-3999 

Accountability for Federal Funds Advanced 5/6/55 

Procedure for Computing the Federal Share of Collections (continued) 

(2) Using total collections for one month of the fiscal year as a sample.--If the percentage representing the Federal share of collections received in any one month in the fiscal year is representative of the Federal share of total collections for the year, such month may serve as the sample period. To determine whether a single month may be used as the sample period, the agency would need to examine its experience with case-by-case computations during the previous year or two. 

If this experience indicates only small variations in month-to-month percentages of the Federal share of collections, the month in which the percentage is closest to the percentage of the Federal share in the total collections for the year may be selected as the sample month.

When the number of collections is small, a State may wish to consider using the total collections for two or three months as a sample period, providing past experience indicates that the months selected will provide a satisfactory basis for distributing the collections. 

Since the percentage derived from the monthly samples may not be known until some time during the year, the agency may use an estimated percentage for computing the Federal share currently. At the end of the year, the agency makes appropriate adjustments for the difference between the estimated percentage and the actual percentage derived from the current year's sample. Or, the agency may wish to apply the results of the sample to a 

12-month period beginning with the month following the sample period.

3345. Federal Participation in Interest on Collections and in Penalties

Imposed by the State for Illegal Payments 

When effecting collections from grantees, some States recover a sum representing the principal amount of assistance paid together with interest and penalties. Federal participation in such collections will be governed by the following considerations: 

1. The Federal Government will participate in all collections of assistance payments and in any interest collected thereon in the same ratio that the Federal Government participated in the assistance payments. 2. 

The Federal Government will not participate in any penalty which may be collected by the State in addition to the amount of assistance and interest recovered.

3. If the full amount of assistance paid (in which the Federal 

Government participated), together with accrued interest is less than the net amount collected, the balance remaining may be considered as a penalty when determining the Federal share of the collection. However, if the net collection is less than the total assistance paid (in which the Federal Government participated) plus accrued interest, no part of the net collection may be considered as a penalty when determining the Federal share of the net collection. 

Adjustments 

In the operation of the public assistance programs it frequently is necessary for States to increase or decrease the amount that may be claimed for Federal participation with respect to an individual payment of assistance. The need for such an adjustment may arise as a result of (a) an overpayment or underpayment of assistance, (b) a refund applicable to a specific payment, (c) administrative review or fiscal audit exceptions, or (d) other incorrect claims for Federal financial participation in specific assistance expenditures. These adjustments may affect the amount of payment, the recipient count or both. 

3346.1 Adjustments for Periods Prior to October 1, 1958 Prior to the Social Security Amendments of 1958, the formula for 

Federal financial participation in money payments was related to individual maximums and the procedures for computing the Federal share of the adjustment were related to the formula in effect at the time of payment. The computation of the Federal share of adjustments covering vendor payments for medical care made between 

July 1, 1957, and October 1, 1958, was related to the formula in effect for that period which was on an average basis.

3400. Support for State Agency's Accounting 3410. Records 

3411. 

Agency records consist of all written material that is prepared or received in the transaction of agency business from which the time, nature, extent, cost, or other data with regard to the transaction can be obtained.

Establishment and Preservation 3411.1 Federal Financial Participation 

As a basis for supporting State claims for Federal participation in expenditures under titles I, IV, X, XIV, and XVI of the Social Security Act, there must be evidence that all requirements have been met which govern Federal participation in assistance payments and administrative costs. This requires that the State agency establish and maintain an organized system of records which will supply this evidence and furnish data for required State agency reports.

The record system necessary for the discharge of the State's accountability must include: Case records which substantiate the agency's finding that individuals receiving payment are eligible within the category to which assistance given is to be charged, and that the computed amount of assistance is in accordance with the State standards; and financial and statistical records and the supporting documents on the basis of which funds are received and expended, and on the basis of which required reports are prepared.

For administrative expenses incurred in providing social services and for staff development, the quarterly claim for Federal financial participation at 75 percent must be based on a State agency's approved cost allocation plan (see V-4050) and must be supported for fiscal audit by the Department of Health, Education, and

Welfare by adequate records, including those specified below: 

1. If the unweighted Classified Case Method is used for allocation of costs of social services (see Handbook V-4052.6.A), the total number of different defined service cases which received a defined service during the quarter will need to be determined 

Transmitted by State Letter No. 660

3411.- p.2

3000-3999 

  

3411.1 Federal Financial Participation (Continued) 

and supported by caseworker day sheets or other equivalent records which identify the cases involved. If the Work Unit 

Method is used (see Handbook V-4052.6.B), the total number of interviews and the number of defined service interviews for defined problem cases are needed and must be supported by caseworker day sheets or other equivalent records which identify the cases involved. If other approved allocation methods are used (see Handbook V-4052.6.C), records similar to those described above must be maintained. 

2. A list or other record is required to identify the personnel whose salaries are charged directly on the 75 percent basis as well as those whose salaries are partly allocated to the 75 percent basis. Use separate lists for staff development and social service personnel. The lists should also show, either directly or by cross reference to other supporting records, the salary amounts and the individual job titles or descriptions and the place of such personnel in the State agency organization. 

Personnel included on the list must be limited to those occupying jobs and performing functions of the type specified in the approved State plan.

State agencies, payments to outside training experts, purchase of training materials, etc.) cross-indexed to the disbursement voucher or other supporting records. The records involved in any claims or expenditures which have been questioned should be further maintained until necessary adjustments have been made and the adjustments have been reviewed and cleared When records are destroyed, the process of destruction must meet the requirements concerning use and disclosure of information regarding public assistance claimants. It is necessary that the agency select a method of disposal for such records that will make it impossible for this record material to fall into the hands of persons not officially responsible to the agency. For example, if records identifying individuals who have applied for or who have received assistance are turned in as paper salvage, they must be previously rendered illegible.

 3411.2 Recommendations 

5/28/63 It is recommended that State procedures relating to the preservation of records of the State public assistance agency be formulated only after careful consideration of the possible need of such records in the administration of the programs for which the agency is responsible. The State agency should satisfy itself as to what are the requirements of its own law in disposing of records, and should provide itself with the necessary standards to guide the staff in putting these requirements into effect. 

Because operating procedures differ widely in the various States, as do the overall State requirements with respect to the preservation of public records, the Bureau has formulated only general standards for the use of all State agencies. The classifications that follow may be useful to State agencies in analyzing the content of their own necessarily more precise standards for disposing of records and in assuring themselves that they have given attention to the major areas of consideration involved in the preservation of records: 

1. Current administrative use 2. 

Criteria should be established by the agency by which "current use" can be determined, particularly as this applies to records which may not be consulted frequently; for example, how often and for what purpose the material is consulted, and whether the transaction of which it was a part has been · entirely completed. 

Financial accountability 

Since the problem of financial accountability for the State is not related to Federal participation only, it may be that records supporting the agency's expenditures will need to be retained longer than is necessary to satisfy accountability to the Federal Government. There are certain financial and eligibility records that are primary records in the sense that they are valid evidence in court of payment or other financial transaction, and these the State will wish to retain for such length of time as is necessary to satisfy the requirements of any specific legislation in the State affecting public agencies, or, in the absence of such specific laws, the statutes of limitation. With respect to public agencies, there is also the possibility of legislative inquiries of various kinds, and State public welfare agencies would 

Transmitted by State Letter No. 660

3411.- p.2 

Part V. 

3000-3999 

5/28/63 

3411.2 Recommendations (Continued) 

5. undoubtedly wish to ascertain from proper State officials what must be preserved to satisfy legislative needs. 

Value for future administrative use, and for use of recipient Some agency records, particularly the case records, have a value for the agency and for the recipient of assistance beyond the current administrative use which the agency may make of them. If the documents used as proof of any factor of eligibility are included in the case record, the State agency will need to consider the value these may have to the individual, their return to him, or their preservation for him. In cases where a former client reapplies, the agency will find it advantageous if it need not repeat all the steps that were necessary to establish his eligibility (or ineligibility) in the first instance. 

Historical and research purposes

Many records of the agency will be of value for historical purposes, for research of various kinds, or for information concerning the administrative process, which may be utilized by future administrators. Records of the agency which have value for these purposes may need to be kept indefinitely. 

State agencies may find that they will benefit by consultation with experts in the field of research, with educational institutions in the State, or with the State archival authority for the purpose of establishing criteria which can be used in determining such value. Consultation with the archival authority may be required by State law, and where this is true, this will, of course, be the first step. 

Maintenance 

Determination of when to keep, summarize, or dispose of records should be based on consideration of availability of storage space and the cost involved in keeping records and the risk of disposing of them. It is necessary to attain some measure of balance between these factors and possible uses of the records. Conceivably, every record of the agency 

Transmitted by State Letter No. 660

3411.p.3 

. 

3000-3999 

3411.2 Recommendations (Continued) 

may have some future use, but the practical aspect of the situation is that records cannot continue to accumulate and that sheer volume may eventually defeat the purpose for which they are kept.

The agency may wish to investigate the microfilm process as an alternative to destruction of records, particularly the older ones which it has been decided to retain. 

This process is being used extensively in business, and in a number of the large Federal agencies, for example, the Social Security Administration, where record keeping is of major importance and hence creates serious security and space problems. At least one State public assistance agency has purchased microfilm equipment and is filming obsolete records, thus releasing hundreds of needed file cabinets as well as considerable space. In both the filming and storage of microfilmed materials, the requirements regarding the use and disclosure of information regarding individuals would need to be observed.

With regard to the problem of maintaining records for indefinite periods, some agencies have experimented with reducing the volume of records, at prescribed intervals, by agency analysis and summary, and disposing of such material as is thought to have temporary rather than permanent value. 

For example, the actual use to be made of information in case records where the individual is reapplying for assistance, or needs his record for other purposes, should determine the criteria for analysis and summary. What record material will reduce the difficulties of the applicant and the public assistance worker in establishing eligibility? Will the lapse of time since prior closing affect this value appreciably?

As time goes on, what information will decrease in value for the individual and the public assistance agency? Can other sources of information serve as well as the record? This method of case-by-case consideration requires considerable staff time. It does reduce the bulk of records, however, and can give assurance of preserving the most pertinent information. 

Transmitted by State Letter No. 660

3411.- p.4 

  

Part V. 

3000-3999 

3411.2 Recommendations (Continued) 

6.

Disposition 

After applying various tests of value, the agency may decide that some records may be disposed of when audits of the period involved have been completed and closed; others will be kept longer as substantiation of the financial expenditures of the agency and to facilitate adjustment where there is likelihood of recovery with respect to assistance payments and administrative costs, particularly those for equipment which may be subject to sale or transfer; some will be kept for future administrative use or for future use in serving individual applicants or recipients; and some records will be kept indefinitely for historical or research purposes. 

Having analyzed its record material in relation to each of these various considerations, the agency will be confronted with another decision, which is, whether it shall keep at a minimum the material within each classification, or shall retain in the agency files everything of possible use. 

Once the necessary administrative decisions have been made, a disposition schedule should be set up, listing specifically how long various classes of record material should be retained in the files, and instructing as to the action to be taken at the expiration of this period. The disposition of records becomes an integral part of the administrative planning of the agency, and it will be possible to evaluate realistically the results of the disposition plan. If the records are disposed of in accordance with a well considered and established schedule, and there is a later need for material that is no longer available, the agency can demonstrate that it has acted in good faith and in accordance with a plan which has been shown by experience to cover all ordinary administrative needs.

3525.11

8/16/19 

Adjustment of Balances 3525.1 Accrual of Balances From Federal Funds.-- 

3525.11

Interpretation.--For the purpose of this statement, approved by the 

Social Security Board on May 1, 1942, balances from Federal funds granted to State public assistance agencies may be classified as 

1. 2. 

Balances from Federal funds remaining after deducting the amount of Federal funds to which the State is entitled on the basis of its expenditures. For convenience, balances of Federal funds falling into this class will be referred to in this statement as "Unearned Balances."

Balances from Federal funds shown on State fund accounts and representing not an excess of Federal grants over the amount to which the State is entitled on the basis of its expenditures, but resulting from the failure on the part of the State to make the necessary transfer of funds or to accomplish the necessary accounting transactions reflecting such transfers so as to take out of the Federal fund accounts any sums to which the State is entitled on the basis of its expenditures. For convenience, balances of Federal funds falling into this class will be referred to in this statement as "Earned Balances." 

There follows a clarification of the above two classifications: 

1. Unearned Balances a. Other than 5 percent addendum

Unearned balances of Federal funds other than those granted under the 5 percent addendum of title I present no particular difficulties. The determination as to whether there is an unearned balance is comparatively simple, since Federal financial participation is on a 50 percent matching basis and the amount of Federal funds to which the State is entitled for a given quarter is readily determined from the total matchable expenditures in that quarter. Nor is there any difficulty encountered in the recovery of such unearned balances since the Social Security Act provides for a quarterly ad justment for the difference, if any, between the amount of the Federal grant for a given quarter and the amount to which the State is entitled on the basis of its expenditures during such quarter. b. 5 percent addendum

Unearned balances of Federal funds granted under the 5 percent addendum provision of title I for the administration of oldage assistance present special problems.

3525.

Past V. 

3000-3999 

8/16/19 

Accrual of Balances From Federal Funds (Continued) 

​• 

Title I, section 3 (a) (2) of the Social Security Act provides that Federal funds granted under the 5 percent addendum".. shall be used for paying the cost of administering the State plan or for old-age assistance, or both, and for no other purpose." The following principles will be observed in determining whether there is an unearned balance of Federal funds granted under the 

5 percent addendum: (1) If the total amount of administrative expenditures for old-age assistance under the State plan in a given quarter is equal to or is greater than the total amount of the Federal 5 percent addendum grant as adjusted for that quarter, it will be considered that there is no unearned balance of the Federal 5 percent addendum grant for that quarter. (2) If the total amount of administrative expenditures for old-age assistance under the State plan in a given quarter is less than the amount of the 5 percent addendum grant, as adjusted for that quarter, but the difference is shown to have been paid as old-age assistance that would be matched, but for the fact that it is paid from this fund or that it is in excess of the Federal maximum, it will be considered that there is no unearned balance of the Federal 5 percent addendum grant for that quarter. (3) If the total amount of administrative expenditures for old-age assistance made under the State plan in a given quarter is less than the amount of the 5 percent addendum grant as adjusted for that quarter, and the difference has not been paid out as old-age assistance in the manner described under (2) above, the remainder will be considered as an unearned balance of the Federal 5 percent addendum for that quarter.

The recovery of unearned balances of Federal 5 percent addendum funds differs from recoveries of other unearned balances of Federal funds for public assistance. In the case of the latter, the Social Security Act provides for a quarterly adjustment for the difference, if any, between Federal funds granted for a given quarter and the amount of Federal funds to which the State is entitled on the basis of the expenditures during such quarter. 

In the case of the 5 percent addendum, however, the act does not

3525.

Part V. 

3000-3999 

  

Accountability for Federal Funds Advanced 8/16/49 

Accrual of Balances From Federal Funds (Continued) 

2. require that such adjustment be based on expenditures made during the particular quarter. This means, in effect, that if during a given quarter the amount granted under the 5 percent addendum for such quarter has not been earned, the unearned balance at the end of such quarter may be available for future State expenditures. A limitation, however, needs to be set up as to the length of time during which 5 percent addendum funds granted in a given quarter and not earned in that quarter may be retained by the State for possible future expenditures.

It has been determined that the time limit for the use of such grants shall be 4 consecutive quarters. This means that if Federal funds granted under the 5 percent addendum provision for a given quarter are not earned in that quarter, the unearned balance of such Federal funds at the end of that quarter may be retained by the State for possible future expenditures until the end of the third quarter following the one for which the funds were granted. If not earned by that time, the balance of such Federal funds will be recovered through adjustment in the next grant for old-age assistance to the State. 

Earned Balances

Earned balances of Federal funds, as previously defined, are funds to which the State is entitled on the basis of its expenditures but which the State failed to transfer from the Federal to the State fund accounts. Earned balances of Federal funds, whether originating from the 5 percent addendum grant or other grants for public assistance under the act, are to be treated in the same manner. The policy, with respect to the accumulation of such Federal fund balances in the State accounts, is that since the State is entitled to these funds, they are no longer Federal funds and the necessary accounting or other action should be taken by the State agency to the end that its accounts reflect the true status of these funds, thus obviating any possible misunderstanding with respect to these funds. Interpretation 

Activities considered to be essential to the efficient administration of public assistance are, in general, the following: 

1. Supervising the operation of the public assistance programs; 

2. Developing, evaluating, and modifying standards of operation; 

3. Maintaining social, financial, and statistical records; 

4. Preparing and presenting information to official bodies and the public; 

5. Determining the original and continued eligibility of individuals for assistance and ascertaining the amount of assistance to be granted;

6. Providing such assistance; 

7. Providing services to applicants and recipients of public assistance that concern the welfare of the individual and assist him to realize the maximum benefit from the assistance payment in relation to personal, family, and community resources.

Requirements Certain broad principles apply to every expenditure for administration for which Federal participation is claimed. In the case of some kinds of administrative expenditures these broad principles are implemented by more detailed conditions governing matching. In all instances, however, the general principles apply.

Financial participation will be available in expenditures of the States for the administration of old-age assistance, aid to dependent children, aid to the blind, and aid to the permanently and totally disabled, if they are: 

1. Essential to the efficient administration of the particular program; reasonably related to the provision of assistance in the particular category; for purposes other than assistance; and not chargeable to another program. 

2. Permissible under State law.

Ordinarily, Federal participation in such expenditures should be claimed in the expenditure report for the quarter in which payment occurs. The State may, however, find it necessary on occasion to delay its claim for Federal financial participation because of some unusual circumstances. This could occur, for example, when delays develop in the preparation of plan material that must be submitted and approved before Federal financial participation may be claimed.

Administrative Expenditures G-4000. Source of State Funds for Administrative Expenditures 

Funds derived from public or private sources deposited in the account of the State or local public assistance agency in accordance with State law are public funds regardless of their source. Such funds may be recognized as State funds subject to Federal financial participation in administrative expenditures, provided the expenditures are made under the State plan and in accordance with Federal policies governing such expenditures as set forth throughout this Handbook. For example, the purchase of social services from another State agency is subject to the policies governing such purchases under the 1962 Public Assistance Amendments (IV-4300, item 

A-2.b).

Public Funds - Funds derived from public sources include: (a) appropriations made directly to the State or local public assistance agency; and (b) allotments, transfers or funds otherwise made available to the State or local public assistance agency by a unit af State or local government. 

Private Funds - Funds derived from private sources include bequests and contributions by private organizations, foundations, agencies, educational or welfare institutions, or individuals. 

Grants from foundations or other private sources in the general field of welfare provide opportunities for State agencies to engage in demonstration projects or to expand their programs to meet needs for which appropriations from public funds are inadequate. Welfare departments should be alert to such possibilities and seek support of public programs from private sources.

Funds from private sources may be made available by the donor for a particular type of activity, such as training, surveys, studies, demonstration projects, administration of social services, etc. For example, funds may be contributed by a voluntary organization, such as a foundation, with the proviso that

these funds be used solely for the training of employees of public assistance' agencies. There may be other bases for ear- ´ marking by the private donor which do not conflict with the basic necessity that the agency have meaningful discretion in the use of the funds. In general, however, no additional re-

No contribution from private sources may be treated as State funds for Federal financial participation which is intended for the training, employing, or aiding of a particular individual or requiring attendance at a particular facility or institution, or which reverts to the donor's facility or use, or to the use of any other designated facility or project.
