Cost Allocation (continued) 1. Direct Charges 

Minimum requirements, applicable only to State agencies administering one or more of the three special types of public assistance in conjunction with programs in addition to old-age assistance, aid to the blind, or aid to dependent children, are as follows: 

Salary and travel expenses

When a section, division, bureau, or department (smallest identifiable organizational group) of a State or of a local or county office is organized on the basis of separate administrative units assigned to a single program or project, all salary expenses and readily identifiable travel expense of each such unit shall be charged directly to that program or project even though minor services of a casual or incidental nature may be performed by such unit for a different program. b. Expenses other than salary and travel (1) For a State office, (2) for a section, division, bureau, or department (smallest identifiable organizational group) of a local office organized on the basis of separate administrative units assigned to a single program or project, and (3) for any local or county office serving an urban community of 100,000 or more population, items of expense in excess of $100 incurred for a single program or project (except those for checkwriting, postage for mailing assistance checks, and telephone and telegraph) shall be charged directly to such program or project. 

Expenses of special projects Direct charges shall be made for expenses of special projects that are not a cost of administration of old-age assistance, aid to dependent children, or aid to the blind--such as expenses incurred for State or local work relief projects, or for storage and distribution of commodities.

State agencies wishing to make additional direct charges of items of expense incurred for a single program or combination of (a) public assistance, and (b) nonpublic assistance programs may do so, provided that a consistent method with respect to all programs or combination of programs is followed.

2. Allocation of Joint Expenses 

Expenses allocated to the three special types of public assistance combined may not exceed an amount which reflects the relative volume of work actually performed on these programs. This amount may be determined in accordance with (a) any of the recommended methods outlined below, or (b) any other method that will reflect the distribution of work among the programs. 

The distribution of joint expenses among the three special types of public assistance may be made on any reasonab‍le basis so long as Federal financial participation is continued on the same matching basis for all three programs.

4041.11 Bases for Distributing Expenditures.--Four bases for distributing expenditures made jointly for two or more programs consistent with the principle of relative volume of work performed on each program are; 1 

1. Total Man-Hour Method 

Under the total man-hour method, time records are maintained by all employees (except employees none of whose working time can be identified with individual programs) on a repetitive basis; such time records show the distribution of the time of each employee according to program. The following procedure is involved in the use of this methods Maintenance of daily time records by program by all employees for a minimum of 2 months in each 6-month period; time recording may be limited to 1 month in 

6 in any local office, when time records are maintained on a staggered basis so that one-sixth of the local offices maintain time records in each month of the 

6-month period.

Determination from time records of the number of manhours devoted to each program during the current period. 

Computation of the percentage of total man-hours applicable to each program. 

2. Work-Unit Method 

Under the work-unit method, units for measuring work performed on each program administered by the agency should be established. In selecting such units, emphasis should be upon "direct" units of "productive" or "operating" employees.

The units selected should be the direct units of work performed by these workers in the investigation of applications and reinvestigation of cases receiving assistance and in rendering social serivces. Examples of such units are: Home visits and collateral visits in the original investigation of applications and cases, and home visits for the purposes of rendering social services.

A-4040. A-4041.1 

Recommendations on Cost Allocation Methods to State Agencies 

Administering Aid to the Permanently and Totally Disabled 

Local Office Expense 

The following is a summary of the modifications necessary under each of the methods recommended in this section of the Handbook for distributing local office expenditures made jointly for two or more programs. 

1. Total man-hour method

State public assistance agencies using the total man-hour method and administering an approved plan for aid to the permanently and totally disabled should add a column for this program to the daily time record. This modification should be introduced by State agencies using either continuous or limited time recording. 

2. Unweighted work-unit method

State public assistance agencies using the unweighted workunit method and administering an approved plan for aid to the permanently and totally disabled should collect information on work units on the same basis as that used for oldage assistance, aid to dependent children, aid to the blind, and other programs. Joint expenses of administration may be distributed among programs on the basis of the percentage distribution of direct units of work (interviews) among programs (including aid to the permanently and totally disabled). 

3. Methods requiring weights for work units, work loads, or case loads (The revisions recommended for the weighted work-load and weighted case-load methods are essentially the same as those for the weighted work-unit method, as described below). 

As in the unweighted work-unit method, State agencies using the weighted work-unit method, should collect work-unit data for all programs, including aid to the permanently and totally disabled. Weights may be derived for all programs

If the agency has been administering general assistance, the weights for this program may be used for aid to the permanently and totally disabled. At the close of the first year of operation of an aid to the permanently and totally disabled program, the weights that have been selected as the "most nearly comparable" should be re-examined and decision reached as to whether these or some other weights should be selected for continuing use. Work units may be recorded on a continuing basis or on a staggered basis, that is, one-sixth of the local units would maintain records of work units for 1 month during each 6-month period.

Work units may be unweighted or weighted for time factors. 

If unweighted work units are used, costs may be allocated among programs on the basis of the percentage distribution of direct units of work (interviews) anong programs. The following procedure is involved in the use of the weighted work-uit method.

Determination from statistical reports of the number of units of work performed on each program by all local offices for each quarterly or semiannual period. b. Establishment of weights reflecting the relative amount of time required on the average for each type of work unit and the ratio of indirect time to time spent on direct units of work by a time study in a selected number of representative counties. These weights may be used as long as they are valid and should be tested from time to time.

Computation of the percentage of weighted work units applicable to each program obtained by (1) weighting the number of work units reported in each program; (2) adding the weighted work umits together by program to obtain total weighted work units in each program; (3) adding the weighted work units in each program to obtain total weighted work units on all programs; and (4) computing the percentage distribution among programs. 

3. Weighted Work-Load Method 

Under the weighted work-load method two work units measuring work load on each program are substituted for the eight work units (interviews) commonly used in the weighted work-unit method; namely, (a) application investigations completed (or certifications and referrals made to other agencies) during the period; and canes reviewed, reinvestigated, or furnished service during the period. The following procedure is involved in the use of this methods

These weights may be used as long as they are valid and should be tested from time to time. (Foto: Information readily available from data yielded by work-unit method.) b. Determination from statistical reports of the number of application investigations completed and the mumber of cases reviewed, reinvestigated, or furnished service, respectively, during the current period. 

Computation of the percentage of weighted work load applicable to each program obtained by (1) weighting the number of application investigations completed and the number of cases reviewed on each program; (2) adding the weighted application investigations to weighted cases reviewed to obtain total weighted work load on each program; (3) adding the weighted work load on each program to obtain total weighted work load on all programs; and (4) computing the percentage distribution among programs. 

4. Weighted Case-Load Method 

Under the weighted casq-load method, only one work umit, cases, is used to measure work load on each program. 

Cases include (a) recipients of assistance or service, and (b) investigations (or referrals) made for other programs providing assistance or service to individuals, such as referrals to State institutions. The following procedure is involved in the use of this method, Establishment of a weight per case for each program on the basis of a time study conducted in representative local units during a selected month or months of each semiannual (preferably) or annual period. Weight per case obtained by dividing total time (both on applications and cases receiving assistance or service) on each program during the period of the time study by the number of cases on each program in the representative local units during the period covered by the time study.

See additional provisions

APTD 4041.11-4041.12

For the initial semiannual or annual period case weights may be obtained from data already available by dividing the proportion of total time or of total work units spent on each program during the most recent semiannual or annual period by the number of cases on the program during this period.

Determination from statistical reports of the average number of cases on each program during the current period. 

Computation of the percentage of weighted case load" applicable to each program obtained by (1) weighting the number of cases on each program, (2) adding the weighted "case loads" on each program to obtain total weighted case load on all programs, and (3) computing the percentage distribution among programs●

Revised methods for the allocation of costs of State and local public assistance agencies for administration, services, and training by program and by function, became necessary as a result of the Public Welfare Amendments of 1962 authorizing 75 percent Federal financial participation in the costs of providing defined social services 1/ and the costs of training in all the Federally aided categories. The Handbook material included in V- 4050-4053 provides the interpretation and the requirements for State plans and for cost allocation procedures related to determining the portion of costs of selected personal services and travel that is subject to 75 percent Federal financial participation.

4052.1 Functions Essential to Efficient Administration 

The functions considered to be essential to the efficient administration of public assistance are, in general, the 

1. 

Supervising the operation of the public assistance programs; 

2. Developing, evaluating, and modifying standards of operation; 

3. Maintaining social, financial, and statistical records; 

4. Preparing and presenting information to official bodies and the public; 

5. Determining the original and continued eligibility of individuals for assistance and ascertaining the amount of assistance to be granted; 

6. Providing such assistance; 

7. Providing services to current, former and potential applicants and recipients of public assistance that concern their welfare, assist them to realize the maximum benefit from the assistance payment, and help them to develop to the maximum their capacity for individual and family functioning. 

4052.2 Costs Subject to 75 Percent Federal Financial Participation 

Costs subject to 75 percent Federal financial participation include (A) functional direct charges for staff development and for defined services, all of which are subject to 75 percent Federal financial participation, and (B) costs of selected personal services and travel, part of which are subject to 75 percent Federal financial participation. These costs are defined as follows:

4052.2 Costs Subject to 75 Percent Federal Financial Participation 

(continued) A. Functional direct charges all of which are subject to 75 percent Federal financial participation are defined as follows: 

1. Functional direct charges for staff development

All or that part of costs chargeable to the Federally aided public assistance programs for: a.

Staff development including personal service and travel for staff development and training staff. b. Agency training sessions in which content deals with public assistance. (1) Costs of operating training centers including salaries, equipment, and rental of space; (2) Expenses of staff while attending agency inservice training; (3) (4) 

Payments for purchase or development of necessary teaching materials and equipment, including books, audio-visual aids, technical devices, etc.; 

Payment to outside experts employed to conduct special courses; payments may include a charge for travel, fee for professional services and per diem; (5) Costs of rental of additional space for inservice training activities.

4052.2 Costs Subject to 75 Percent Federal Financial Participation continued)

C. 

Persons preparing for employment in public assistance.

Payments made directly to individuals or to an educational institution on behalf of such individuals for costs of education in preparing for employment in public assistance. Such expenses include tuition and fees, costs of books, costs of instruction, cost of travel to and from the institution attended, living costs of the individual in the school community, and other needs of the individual and his dependents. Payments may be made in a lump sum or in installments to cover an item or a group of items. d. Field instruction units. e.

Instructional costs of public assistance field instruction units, including: rental of space, salaries, travel to and from field work units, clerical assistance, and teaching materials and equipment, such as books and audio-visual aids necessary for effective instruction. 

Educational leave. 

(1) Direct payments to employees on educational leave in an amount not to exceed salary plus the additional costs of obtaining the education; (2) Payments made directly to employees or to an educational institution on behalf of employees for costs of education. Such expenses include tuition and fees, cost of books, cost of instruction, cost of travel to and from the institution attended, living costs of the individual and his dependents. Payments may be made in a lump sum or in installments to cover an item or a group of items.

Method A.

State and local staff development personnel..

Agency training sessions in which content deals with public assistance.--Charge full amount to 

Federally aided public assistance programs.

Persons preparing for employment in public assistance.--Charge full amount to Federally aided public assistance programs.

For field units providing instruction for both child welfare and public assistance, use currently approved allocation method to separate child welfare costs and charge the balance to 

Federally aided public assistance programs. 

5. Educational leave.--Charge to Federally aided public assistance programs the full amount of expenditures incurred for employees whose primary job will be in public assistance administration upon return from educational leave.

4052.7 Definitions 

A. Cases receiving defined services will include: 

1. Cases of current applicants for and recipients of assistance payments. 8.

Cases for which a social study has been made during the quarter to the point of determining the presence or absence of current specified problems.

Transmitted by State Letter No. 606

4052.- p.2 

  Administrative Expenditures 

11/30/62 

Part V. 

4000-4999 

4052.7 Definitions (continued) 4053 2. b. 

Cases identified, either through a social study or otherwise, as having need for a defined service with respect to a current specified problem and for which a defined service was provided during the quarter.

Service only cases, that is former or potential *772 85000 applicants for and recipients of public assistance who received defined services during the quarter. 

B. Defined service is an interview by the direct service staff focused on a current specified problem; it must have been of sufficient importance to have been recorded in the case record.

C. D. An interview is defined as a purposeful contact, either face-to-face or by telephone, with the client or with a collateral person in the office or elsewhere of sufficient importance to have been recorded in the case record. 

A defined service interview is an interview that meets the definition in item C above and, in addition, is focused on a current specified problem.

Requirements for State Plans 4053.1 Cost Allocation 

For purposes of claiming Federal funds, a State plan must include a cost allocation plan that provides for (1) distinguishing the costs of administering the special types of public assistance from all other administrative costs of the agency in such a manner that no part of the cost of administering other programs is charged to the special types of public assistance; (2) allocating the costs of administering the special types of public assistance among programs on a reasonable basis; and (3) determining within each 

Federally aided public assistance program the amount that is subject to 75 percent Federal financial participation and the amount subject to 50 percent Federal financial participation. 

Transmitted by State Letter No. 606

4053.3 Determination of Costs Subject to 75 Percent Federal Financial 

Participation A. 

Functional direct charges

A State plan that provides for functional direct charges of costs subject to 75 percent Federal participation must meet the following conditions: 1. 2. 

Functional direct charges, i.e., costs identified in their entirety with staff development (except for those allocable to the program of child welfare services) and with provision of defined services (prescribed and specified) must meet the conditions specified in III-3000 and IV-4000, respectively.

Functional direct charges for provision of defined service by personnel described in V-4052.2 item A. 

2b can be made only if the State plan for social services provides for: a. b. 

A standard for visits to families and adults with specified problems that provides for visits at least at intervals not to exceed three months. 

Caseload standards (1) For AFDC, in States electing to include for defined services all AFDC families.--A caseload standard not to exceed 60 cases per worker and a supervisory standard not to exceed 5 workers per supervisor. Fart V. 4000-4999 4100.

  Administrative Expenditures Goods, Facilities, and Services From Other State Agencies 4110. General Requirements 

4111. 4112. 8/4/47 

Effective November 5, 1946, matching of State expenditures will be available under titles I,IV, and X of the Social Security Act to meet the costs incurred by State agencies in furnishing goods, facilities, or services to a State public assistance agency provided: 

1. Such costs are permissible under State law; 2. 3. 

Such costs are incurred to meet the administrative needs of the State agencies and are not costs attributable to the general expense of State government in carrying out the overall, coordinating, fiscal, and administrative functions of the State government; and 

Such costs are extra, identifiable, and readily ascertainable either by (a) segregation or (b) as a pro rata share of the cost of such goods, facilities, or services. 

Definitions of Terms.--"Goods" means articles or commodities such as furniture, equipment, printed forms, office supplies, wares, or merchandise. "Facilities" means transportation and communications, such as automobile and motor delivery facilities, and telephone and telegraph. Those facilities that may be used in comection with office space, such as heat, light, power, insurance, elevator service, janitor service, cleaning, painting, decorating, and maintenance repairs are excluded from this definition and are covered by a separate policy. (See V-4500.) The printing, writing, and mailing of public assistance checks, or any combination of such operations, including both services and goods, may be classified as a facility. "Services" relates to personal services performed by employees of other State departments, including costs of travel, and supplies, necessary and directly related to the performance of the personal service rendered. 

Criteria for Application of Requirements.--As an aid in applying this policy it is necessary that consideration be given to the criteria set forth below under all three of the principles:

- Part V. 

L000-4999 

  Administrative Expenditures 

84747 

 1. Such Costs Are Permissible Under State Law 

2. 

Costs for which matching is available must be incurred in accordance with the requirements of the State law. 

Such Costs Are Incurred to Meet the Administrative Needs of the State Agencies and Are Not Costs Attributable to the General Expense of State Government in Carrying Out the Over-All, Coordinating, Fiscal, and Administrative 

Functions of the State Government 

The costs of goods, facilities, or services provided by other State agencies or departments will be considered to be incur red to meet the administrative needs of the public assistance agency when the goods, facilities, or services are of such character that the public assistance agency would otherwise have had to provide them as a part of its own agency administration. Some States may find it economical to centralize certain operations which are common to many State agencies, such as accounting, purchasing, and personnel administration. Charges for services, goods, or facilities so provided, may include only the costs of the goods, facilities, or services themselves and must exclude costs of general management and supervision of the other State departments, as well as costs of functions not related to public assistance administration. In arriving at a proper lump-sum charge, or a unit cost charge, as, for example, for check-writing, the cost of certain "feeder" operations, such as filing and checking, may be included with the cost of materials directly used, the cost of the services of persornel directly engaged, and the cost of immediate supervision of such personnel provided such "feeder" operations are reasonably related to the operation for which a charge is made. In determining what agency operations may be included in computing the cost chargeable to the public assistance agency, the State should not follow back into the servicing department's administration to such a degree that over-all costs are included. 

The costs of services provided by other State agencies or departments will be considered to be incurred by the State in carrying out the over-all coordinating, fiscal, and administrative functions of State government when such services are to maintain controls, make inspections, or otherwise to assure the State of conformity to general provisions of State law and regulations, and to perform research

4112 

Part V. 

4000-4999 

Fiscal Operations and Acoountability 

Administrative Expenditures  

4113. 3. 8/4/47 studies or other activities concerned with control and management of State government. Departments established to carry out the sovereign functions of the State may also provide certain specified and identifiable services for other State agencies. Where this dual responsibility exists, only the extra identifiable costs properly attributable to the public assistance agency are matchadlo. 

Such Costs are Extra, Identifiable, and Readily As certainable 

Either by Segregation, or (b) as a Pro Rate Share of the Cost of Such Goods, Facilities, or Services 

The use of the term "extra, identifiable" in this principle means that the goods, facilities, or services must be "extra" in the sense that they required an identifiable additional expense to or work load of a State or local department to supply them to the public assistance agency and are not the costs of goods, facilities, or services that the supplying department otherwise would have had to incur. 

Illustrations of Application of Requirements.--The following are illustrative examples of the application of the principles above:

1. 

Governor's Office 

The expenses of maintaining the chief executive of the State and the functions of the governor's office obviously belong to the general category of costs arising in maintaining the State government. Such expenses are a primary requisite of the State government itself, and would not ordinarily be attributable in whole or in part, as costs of services to the public assistance agency. The governor, as the administrative head of the State, is responsible for exercising the function of general coordinating and control powers. In such capacity, the expenses of his office would not be incurred for the proper and efficient administration of the public assistance programs. 

2. Attorney General 

Services performed by the ettorney general would, in general, be matchable if performed as an aid to the operation of the State public assistance agency, the service being of a kind for which the agency would normally turn to its own staff attorney. Bervices performed by the State attorney general

Part V. 

4000-4999 

 . 

Administrative Expenditures 

10/13/47 

in his capacity of law officer of the State are not matchable. 

Only the direct costs of those services which are specifically identifiable as services to the public assistance agency are matchable. Whether such services are performed in the attorney general's office, or on the premises of the agency is immaterial. The nature and identifiability of the service governs the matchability. In all instances the services from the State attorney general would need to be known in some detail so that those that are matchable could be separated and identified from those that may be performed by the State attorney general in his capacity of law officer of the State. 

Certain specifio services, for example, informal consultation on policies and procedures or assistance in drafting proposed legislation, would be matchable. In this regard the State attorney general would be acting in an advisory capacity to the State agency in the same way as an attorney on the agency's staff. It is also possible that he might consult with the 

State agency on fair hearing procedures, and review records in order to advise the State agency as to the sufficiency of evidence of other legal points raised by the hearing. These services also would be matchable.

Services that are performed by the State attorney general and that fall within the general administrative responsibilities of his office would not be matchable, as, for example, formal opinions on the constitutionality of legislation, defending the State agency in nonprogram litigation, or the rendering of opinions as a part of the general function of the State attorney general's office. Such services usually would be required of the State attorney general for all the State agencies and departments and would not be performed solely for the public assistance State agency. The interpretative material beginning with V-4111 contains criteria that were developed to explain the application of the three principles of the policy. No additional guides have been prepared, and it is presumed that further generalization as to the functions of State government and their matchability is not feasible. With the criteria contained in the interpretative material it will now be necessary to look at each Stato situation and apply the three principles of the policy to the specific activities of State government involved. Such review will determine whether the conditions of the policy are met and whether the costs are attributable to the public assistance program.

Part V. 

4.000-4999 

10/13/47 

 Administrative Expenditures  

Under V-4113 charges should be confined to the cost to the State attorney general of the time of the personnel, of materials used, and of expenses incurred as the direct result of furnishing the services. Overhead, such as office rent, library facilities, or the cost of general management or supervision could not be included. However, the value of time spent personally by a supervisor in rendering services could, of course, be used in the computation. Probably it would be necessary for each individual who renders such services to keep a record of the time consumed, from which the cost to the 

State public assistance agency could be determined.* 

3. Department of Finance

In the case of the State department of finance, the cost of every activity performed as a service to the public assistance agency which the agency would otherwise itself perform is a matchable cost. The chief test of whether or not the costs were incurred to meet the administrative needs of the public assistance agency would be to determine if the agency could have dispensed with the service without detriment to the proper and efficient administration of the programs. The second test would be to determine if overhead and other indirect costs of management and general supervision have been excluded by the State public assistance agency in making its claim for matching. 

4. Treasurer

The expenses of the State treasurer, like those of a State department of finance, are a combination of costs in the exercise of the treasurer's activity as the guardian of State funds and in the providing of special fiscal services to the departments of the State. Those costs that are identifiable and arise from the administrative needs of the public assistance agency, as, for example, check-writing, would be matchable. Those broad functions of the treasurer's office inherent in the guardianship of State funds and the accountability of funds held in his custody would not involve costs attributable to the operation of the public assistance programs.

 5. State Civil Service 

1 In the case of State civil service agencies, "extra, identifiable, as defined in V-L112, principle 3, is interpreted to permit financial participation on a prorated or segregated basis in the proper and reasonable cost of providing services of the kind needed by the State public assistance agency to carry out the "Standards for a Merit System of Personnel Admin- H. 52 istration." i renversée III-2000 ) Under these standards, certain merit system activities are required to be performed by an administrative unit separate from the State public assistance agency, and as performed for the State public assistance agency must be in compliance with the provisions and conditions in the approved State plan. proper prorata share of the total costs, including overhead, of such services, where they are performed by a State civil service agency, may be considered "extra, identifiable" as this term is used in principle 3. The method of prorating must be reasonable and assure that the costs of services and related overhead which are not needed to carry out the standards are excluded from the costs charged to titles I, IV, and X. 8..

Alternative bases of Federal participation in costs of 

State civil service agency

Federal participation in the costs of a State civil service agency may be determined on either of the following bases: (1) Costs of special services (a) In the direct cost, excluding overhead, of a special service to the State public assistance agency as, for example, the cost of an examination for a class of positions occurring only in the public assistance agency, and/or (b) In a prorata cost of a special service, excluding overhead, in which the public assistance agency shares with other State agencies, as, for example, the cost of an examination and the establishment and use of a register which will be used to fill positions in the public assistance agency and in other State agencies.

Part V. 

4000-4999 

  Administrative Expenditures 

4/15/48 

(2) Costs of continuing services (a) In a prorata share of the total costs incurred by the State civil service agency in rendering the services needed to carry out the personnel standards performed as a continuing service to the State public assistance agency, including that part of the over-all administrative costs properly chargeable to such services. (b) In a prorata share of the costs incurred by the State civil service agency in rendering one or more of the services needed to carry out the personnel standards performed as a continuing service for the State public assistance agency, including that part of the over-all administrative costs properly chargeable to the service or services rendered.

Method of prorating the costs of continuing services The method of prorating the costs incurred by the State civil service agency should be such that the share prorated to the State public assistance agency is appropriately related to the services regularly available to the State public assistance agency and to other State agencies being served. In general, either of the following would afford a satisfactory basis of prorating the matchable costs: (1) The ratio that the number of budgeted positions under the State civil service system in the State public assistance agency bears to the total number of budgeted positions under the State civil service system in all State departments; or (2) The ratio that the amount of the pay roll for such positions in the State public assistance agency bears to the total pay roll for positions under the State civil service system in all 

State departments.

The State public assistance agency, if it prefers, may submit for approval some other method of prorating the costs of continuing services incurred by the State civil service agency, with such justification as is necessary to establish that it provides a reasonable basis for prorating a share of the total matchable costs to the State public assistance agency. b. Federal participation in costs of State civil service agency without direct legislative appropriation

Where a State public assistance agency requests matching in a prorata share of the costs of continuing services, there must be, over a period of time, a reasonable relation between the distribution of the services performed by the State civil service agency and the distribution of the costs attributed to such services in order to justify a continuation of prorata sharing of such costs. The State public assistance agency will need to maintain a record of the type, quantity, and date of services rendered in order to assure itself that the expenditures incurred by the Státe civil service agency in which the public assistance agency claims matching, over a period of time, are reasonably related to the programs and essential to their efficient operation. (The effective date of this paragraph is November 5, 1946.)

4114. 4115. 

Accounting for Expenditures.--The accounting records of State and local public assistance agencies will need to contain data supporting charges by other State agencies. These data should reflect such information as: the identity of the department supplying the goods, or facilities, or performing the service; the amount involved; the type and quantity of goods, facilities, and services furnished; the basis for determining the amount; and an approval by the public assistance agency. This documentation in support of the State's claim will be reviewed in the audit of State public assistance expenditures. On occasion, it may be necessary to examine in the office of the supplying State department those evidences of disbursement which support the amount of the expenditure. It is recommended but not required that unit costs be determined for all nonsegregable types of costs of other State departments. The regional staff of the Social Security Administration is available to assist State public assistance agencies in defining units of measurement, procedures for establishing the cost of service per unit, or in devising formulae for allocating portions of costs incurred by other State departments to the public assistance agencies.

Transfer of Earned Federal Funds.--Costs of goods, facilities or services provided by other public agencies that are claimed by the State public assistance agency as costs incurred for the administration of public assistance will ordinarily appear as an expenditure in the accounting records of the public assistance agency. Where the claim for Federal participation is based on a disbursement by another agency and the expenditure therefor is not entered on the books of the State public assistance agency, the Federal funds received as a result of the State's claim nevertheless usually will be entered on the public assistance agency's books as earned Federal funds and the agency's accounting records will ordinarily show either: (1) a disbursement in a corresponding amount to the agency that provided the service and incurred the expenditure; or, (2) a book transfer of the amount to the central State financial authority.

Where the State public assistance agency plans to retain the Federal funds earned as a result of another State or local agency's expenditure, the public assistance agency may nevertheless claim Federal participation in such expenditure

4250. Automatic Data Processing (ADP) 

4251. General 4251.1 Purpose 

This policy prescribes the conditions under which Federal financial par ipation will be available in public assistance administrative costs for (1) the acquisition of automatic data processing equipment, (2) the continued use of automatic data processing equipment delivered prior to July 1, 1965, or (3) the purchase of automatic data processing services. 4251.2 Scope 

The equipment affected by the policy statement herein encompasses: 8. b. c.

Electronic computers, irrespective of size, capacity or cost

All peripheral or auxiliary equipment used in support of electronic computers, whether or not cable connected and whether selected and acquired with the computer or separately Punched card equipment only when used in conjunction with an electronic computer. 

4251.3 Definition of Terms 

Data Processing is defined as the preparation of source media which contain data or basic elements of information, and the handling of such data according to precise rules of procedure to accomplish such operations as classifying, sorting, calculating, summarizing, and recording.

Automatic Data Processing (ADP) is defined as data processing performed by a system of electronic or electrical machines SO interconnected and inter-acting as to reduce to a minimm the need for human assistance or intervention.

Automatic Data Processing Equipment (ADP Equipment) is defined as a machine or group of interconnected machines consisting of 

H.T. No. 51

4251.3-p. 2 

4000-4999 

 Administrative Expenditures 

3/5/65 

4251.3 Definition of Terms (continued) 

input, storage, computing, control, and output devices which perform arithmetic and/or logic operations automatically by means of internally stored or externally controlled programme d instructions.

A Probability Study is a relatively broad gauged and brief study to determine whether it is sufficiently probable that effective use of AIP equipment can be made to warrant the substantial investment of staff, time, and money in a more detailed study. 

Systems Analysis is the examination in greater detail of the functions and procedures in order to obtain an understanding of the system as it exists. The analysis describes existing procedures, exposes deficiencies, and enables a determination of what the real needs are for improving present systems or developing new ones. The analysis also includes information on the cost of the existing system in order to provide a basis for comparison with the estimated costs of the new improvements. 

Systems Design is the putting together of a new and more efficient system which avoids the deficiencies and discrepancies discovered in the old system and capitalizes on the capabilities of the equipment. The systems design includes also the development of systems specifications which provide a basis for the selection of equipment.

A New Installation is the acquisition of automatic data processing equipment (see definition) by a welfare agency where such a system did not exist previously; or the initial purchase of ADP services by a public assistance agency where such a service was not secured previously.

Change in Existing ADP Equipment is the acquisition of additional automatic data processing equipment (see definition) or the replacement of existing ADP equipment subsequent to a submittal of information which has been made in accordance with the requirements of this policy. 

H.T. No. 51

Part V 

4000-4999 

 Fiscal Operations and Accountabili Administrative Expenditures 

3/5/65 

4252. Conditions for Federal Financial Participation 4252.1 Equipment Under the Control and Supervision of the State or Local Welfare Agency 

Under conditions specified below, Federal financial participation is available in the cost of ADP equipment operated by a public assistance agency under its control and supervision. For the purpose of this policy the acquisition of such equipment is classified into three groups, (A) for new installations, 1.e., where the ADP equipment is delivered on or after July 1, 1965, (B) existing ADP installations, i.e., where such equipment has been delivered prior to July 1, 1965, and (C) changes in existing ADP equipment, i.e., acquisition of additional equipment or replacement of existing equipment on or after July 1, 1965. A.

New Installations 

Federal financial participation in State and local agency expenditures, under plans approved under the public assistance titles of the Social Security Act, to meet the costs of rental, lease-purchase, or purchase, by a State or local welfare agency, of ADP equipment delivered on or after July 

1, 1965, is available, provided: 

1. The following studies have been made 2. a. b.

A probability study which shall encompass, as a minimum, a brief examination of each process performed by the agency within specified general welfare areas (see V-4252.1, item A3b).

A study in greater depth, i.e., a systems analysis and systems design, of each of the welfare processes that the agency plans to convert to ADP in the initial application as well as of those processes which are interrelated.

3/5/65 Under conditions specified below, a public assistance agency may secure Federal financial participation in the purchase of services from ADP units that are operated and controlled by other units of State or local government or from commercial or other non-public facilities. For purposes of this policy, such purchase agreements are classified into three groups, (A) new proposals and agreements entered into on or after July 1, 1965, (B) existing contracts, agreements, or arrangements in force prior to July 1, 1965, and (C) changes in existing contracts, agreements or arrangements. (It is not intended that the following requirements will apply in the case of occasional purchase of ADP services for the processing of one-time or other special reports.)

A. New Proposals or Agreements Relative to the Purchase of ADP Services 

Federal financial participation in State and local agency expenditures, under plans approved under the public assistance titles of the Social Security Act, to meet the cost of ADP services purchased on or after July 1, 1965, by a State or local welfare agency from another governmental unit or from commercial or other non-public facilities is available only under the following conditions: 

1. Requirements of Handbook V-4100 have been met for the purchase of services from other governmental agencies. 

2. A study in depth, 1.e., systems analysis and design, has been made of each welfare process for which ADP services will be purchased and of each of the processes which are interrelated (see V-4251.3). 

3. The study referred to in item 2 and conclusions, if prepared by a local welfare agency, another agency of the State or local government or private consultants, etc., have been reviewed and approved by the State agency.

4430. 4430.1 4432. 4432.1 4432.2 Travel Purpose.--Costs incurred by State and local public assistance agencies for intrastate and interstate travel, and, in certain circumstances, for travel to meetings outside the United States, necessary to the proper and efficient administration of the State public assistance programs, will be subject to Federal financial participation. Interpretation General.-In the administration of the public assistance programs, travel expenses necessarily will be incurred by representatives of the State and local public assistance agencies. The State agency will be expected to comply with State statutes and regulations governing requirements for travel, and, when applicable, with local statutes and regulations of any particular area of the State. For example, the 

State agency would need to comply with regulations pertaining to the conditions of travel, designation of official station, use of official or personal car, mileage or other rates, subsistence, per diem, flat travel allowances, and the maintenance of appropriate records in support of travel performed. If such regulations are not in existence or do not cover completely the agency operations, the State agency will need to establish requirements governing expenditures for all types of travel to be performed. The regulations of the State public assistance agency should be consistent with those of other agencies of the State. The cost should be reasonable and related to the purpose of the travel.

Foreign Travel.--As social problems of an international character develop and participation by public officials in international affairs increases, a State may consider certain foreign travel as a function of the administration of the public assistance programs. There are intergovernmental and other international meetings which representatives of State public assistance agencies may attend. In intergovernmental meetings, official United States representatives are designated by the Department of State. This department serves as the official 

Federal approving agency for persons who are to act as representatives of the United States of America. These persons are "representatives" in the sense that they would present the national point of view in a particular field, and could obtain a benefit from such meetings that would increase their usefulness in their field. The expenses of these persons are, in some cases, borne by the United States Department of State. In other instances, the Department of State may designate a person as an official United States representative, but travel

4432.2.1 

  Administrative Expenditures 

3/19/52 

Part V 

4000-4999 

4433.1 expenses are borne by the employing agency or met personally by the delegate. In nongovernmental international meetings, representatives may be designated by a recognized organized group in the United States or by a State public assistance agency, and the expenses may be met out of the funds of that group or agency. In order that the Bureau of Public Assistance may have advance information of State agencies' activities in international affairs, it is desirable that, for all foreign travel, notice of designation as a representative be submitted to the appropriate regional office. Expenses for travel--such as to confer with officials of countries that border the United States, concerning local community matters of public welfare interest-ordinarily would be considered for Federal participation on the same bases as costs of domestic travel and presumably would be subject to State regulation governing such travel. 

Requirements for State Plans.--The State plan must identify and briefly summarize the pertinent rules and regulations governing State and local travel affecting the operation of the public assistance agency, and include those that the State agency needs to develop on conditions of travel, designation of official station, use of official or personal car, mileage or other rates, subsistence, per diem, flat travel allowances, and the forms and procedures relating to the support of travel expenditures. State laws, rules, and regulations of State-wide applicability governing travel need not be submitted, but should be cited in the plan, with appropriate statements to identify their content and their applicability. Copies of the essential documents must be available in the office of the State agency for use in the review of public assistance expenditures.

If the State agency proposes to pay flat travel allowances, the plan must so specify, and must show that: (1) such allowances are permissible under State law, and that State statutes do not require the accounting of costs on the basis of mileage, per diem, or actual expenses (2) the allowances are related objectively to the actual travel performed; (3) the State agency will test the validity of the allowances from time to time; and (4) appropriate adjustments will be made in the amounts when the circumstances indicate that conditions affecting the employee's allowance for travel or the objective relationship of the allowances to actual travel experience has materially changed. 152

4433.2 

4000-4999 

 Administrative Expenditures 

3/19/52 

4433.2 4434.

Federal Financial Participation.--Federal financial participation will be available in expenses of travel, including subsistence while in travel status and foreign travel, incurred in administration of State public assistance plans when such travel is authorized in writing by the appropriate approving official, is for an appropriate purpose, and of specified duration (continuous or intermittent) in accordance with the following requirements: 

1. Travel expenses must have been incurred in accordance with the rates and conditions set forth in the State statutes and Statewide regulations, or in State agency regulations not in conflict therewith, or in local statutes and regulations approved by the State agency for any particular area of the State. 2. 

For travel to international meetings, in addition to the State's approval, the State agency must show that there has been prior clearance and status designation by the Department of State as a representative for intergovernmental conferences, or a designation by a sponsoring national organization or State agency as a repr sentative for nonintergovernmental conferences.

Recommendations.--The necessity for traveling by car to visit applicants and recipients of public assistance makes the use of a car almost mandatory upon a visitor. The recruitment of qualified workers has frequently been adversely affected wherever the public assistance agencies have required ownership of a car as a condition of employment. 

State agencies may purchase cars for use in performing travel that is essential to the administration of public assistance. Federal par- ticipation is available in such purchase cost, as well as in the costs of gas, oil, repair, and other costs of operating the vehicle while used on official business. If State cars are used by staff of more than one State agency, appropriate charges may be made to the public assistance agency, and Federal participation may be available for such cost.

3. Identification of the agency responsible for the administration of the benefit system and the basis for prorating the costs, including administrative costs, if any, among the participating agencies of the State.

Federal Financial Participation.--Federal financial participation is available in State expenditures for the costs of employee benefit plans, including costs of administration, when such charges are made on a fair and reasonable basis as compared to that applied to other agencies of the State covered by the law. Federal financial participation will be available: 

1. When State or local systems are established in accordance with law or ordinance, provided: a. The law requires that all employees of the State or local public assistance agency be covered by the system, or, if the law is elective, permits any employee to have such protection if he so desires, except that if the law establishes specified coverage groups or excludes certain types of positions or persons--such as part-time consultants, casual workers, and the like--Federal participation will be available only if the exclusion or the definition of coverage groups is determined to be reasonable. b. The rate of contribution (or allocated cost) is calculated on the same basis for all employees of State or local agencies covered by the system or, if more than one basis is authorized by the law, the basis used for the State public assistance agency is fair and reasonable as compared to that applied to other agencies. C.

Contributions to the benefit fund and earnings thereof may be used solely for the payment of benefits, including lump-sum payments, to individuals entitled thereto under the law, or for the administration of the law, and may not be diverted to other uses. 

2. When, under section 218 of title II of the Social Security Act, the Federal Security Administrator has entered into an agreement with any State to extend the insurance coverage of this title to employees of the State or its local subdivisions. Any exclusion of services which is permitted and any exclusion of positions which is required under section 218 will be regarded as reasonable.

4440. Employee Benefit Plans 

4440.01 General.--Various kinds of employee benefit plans in operation in the 

States afford protection to the employees of State and local public assistance agencies. Through the use of such benefit plans, the State recognizes that these employees are entitled to economic security in old age, unemployment, employment accidents, ill health, and disability. Such measures for protection are important in sound administration. 4443.1 

Under titles I, IV, X, and XIV of the Social Security Act, Federal funds are available to State public assistance agencies for the costs of employee benefit plans, such as State and local retirement systems including old-age and survivors insurance, workmen's compensation, unemployment compensation, health and hospital benefits, provided such expenditures are not contrary to applicable Federal or State law and are in accord with approved State plans as necessary for the operation of the public assistance program. 

Requirements for State Plans.-To secure Federal financial participa tion in the cost of employee benefit plans, the State public assistance agency must identify and briefly summarize the regulations and policies under which the particular benefit program operates. Applicable laws and State-wide policies and regulations establishing such benefit plans need not be submitted, but appropriate citations and interpretations of such laws will be necessary. State plan material must include the following: 

1. Summary of the provisions that relate to the operation of the benefit plans, such as the extent of coverage of State and local employees; the rate of contribution; and the scope and kind of benefits provided under the plan. In the case of coverage of 

State agency employees under old-age and survivors insurance, this summary may be limited to a statement that such an agreement with the Social Security Administration has been executed, with the effective date or identification number of the agreement. 

Effective date of the law or agreement as applicable to employees of the State or local agencies and the effective date of claiming 2. 

Federal financial participation.

Office Space 

4501. 4510. 

General.--In the administration of the public assistance titles of the Social Security Act, it is the responsibility of the State public assistance agency to secure and maintain the type of housing that will meet adequately and efficiently its particular needs in the administration of the aid programs. Expenditures for providing adequate office space for State and local public assistance operations will generally be expenses for which Federal financial participation may be claimed under titles I, IV, X, and XIV of the act.

The costs of office space may be claimed for amounts incurred in (1) paying rent and service and maintenance costs in privately owned buildings, (2) meeting the costs of service and maintenance in lieu of rent in publicly owned buildings, (3) meeting rental charges in municipally and Federally owned buildings, (4) making repairs and alterations to either privately or publicly owned buildings, and (5) monthly rental charges, based on the cost of initial construction or purchase of State or locally owned buildings. All such expense must represent a benefit to the State agency and be supported as an actual cost of the State agency or as an outlay incurred by the State or local government in providing office space. Such costs must be based on an actual rental charge or a monthly rental rate that is a reasonable approximation of actual cost over a long-run period. Regardless of the elements considered in determining the cost, when claimed, the Federal Government participates only on a montaly basis for periods when the State agency occupies space and receives Federal grants, and the rate claimed for any type of costs or combination may not exceed the actual cost and must be within comparable rental in the particular community. All rental charges clained by the State public assistance agencies will be reviewed periodically to assure that the Federal Government is not participating in any expenses that are in excess of actual cost. 

Standard of Comparable Rental 

The principal guide in establishing the basis for Federal financial participation in the costs of office space is the relation of such expense to the comparable commercial rental of the particular locality. This basic principle of relating charges to comparable rental cannot be overemphasized as a guide in assuring that Federal funds are not used to reimburse States for costs that exceed the rental rate for comparable space in the same or 

Transmitted by State Letter No. 236 

159

4510 

 Fart V. 

4000-4999 

 Administrative Expenditures 

12/23/54 

4520. similar locality. Although comparable rental is not a fixed amount for any particular locality and the rental rates will vary within a locality as well as between localities, it is, nevertheless, expected that a realistic determination of the comparable rental be made. The State agency will generally need to seek reliable and expert advice in establishing the comparable rental and should secure at least three statements from such sources to support a determination. For example, real estate appraisers, State or county purchasing officers, State and local real estate boards, county assessors, private realtors, and other individuals who may have special knowledge of rental rates should be of considerable service in providing an authoritative determination of the comparable rental for a particular locality. The State agency may have data on comparable rental for certain localities through its experience in paying rents in privately owned buildings. 

Such cost figures might indicate a range of comparable rental for certain types of localities and would be useful, as a general guide, in evaluating appraisals of comparable rental for publicly owned buildings. When it is difficult to establish the comparable rental for a particular locality, comparisons can be made with the cost of space of equal convenience in another locality of the State where population and economic factors affecting the rental rates are similar. In all instances, in inking comparisons the State agency would compare rates that are based on like elements of cost. For example, rental charges which include service and maintenance costs should not be compared with the rates that do not include such expenses.

Ratio of Service and Maintenance Costs to Commercial Rental Rates 

Experience in the analysis of the component elements of cost in commercial rental rates has shown that approximately 75 percent of such rates can be identified as an expense of service and maintenance including depreciation. The portion of such charges in excess of 75 percent of the comparable rental rate would represent taxes and the margin of profit on the investment that would ordinarily accrue in a private rental arrangement. Consequently, whenever a charge is made for space in a publicly owned building that is not in excess of 75 percent of the comparable rental for the same or similar locality, it may be assumed that 

Transmitted by State Letter No. 236

4520 

Part V. 

4000-4999 

  Administrative Expenditures  

4531. 4532. 12/23/54 such cost is reasonably related to the expense necessary to maintain the building for occupancy. This is especially true if the proposed charge does not exceed 75 percent of the lowest appraisal of the comparable rental that the State agency may obtain.

The measure of 75 percent of the lowest appraisal of the comparable rental can thus be used to advantage by the State agency and the 

Social Security Administration, in simplifying the review and approval of requests for Federal financial participation in the costs of office space in publicly ovmed buildings. The use of this standard will enable the State agency, under certain circumstances, to claim costs that are less than 75 percent of the lowest appraisal of comparable rental without prior review or approval by the Social Security Administration. The basis of this type of claim has been provided under Federal Financial 

Participation (V-4532-4532.51).

Requirements for State Plans.--The State plan must indicate whether the State agency intends to claim Federal financial participation in expense for State or local office space. 

The plan should clearly indicate the scope and basis of such requests, For purposes of securing financial participation the plan must state: 1. 2. 

The types of cost that will be incurred for cfice space, such as private rentals, costs of service and maintenance, repairs and alterations, charges in municipal and Federal buildings, or rental charges based on the initial costs of construction and purchase; and

The method of determining the amount of the rental charges, the relationship of the charges to comparable rental, and the basis for State agency approval of local office space expenditures.

Federal Financial Participation.--Federal financial participation is available in the costs of office space for State or local public assistance agencies in privately or publicly owned buildings. 

The principles that govern participation in such costs are:

Requirements for specific types of office space expense follow: 

Rent in Privately Owned Buildings.--Federal financial participation is available in private rental costs, including expenses of service and maintenance and repairs and alterations that do not exceed the rate for equivalent space and facilities in accordance with the comparable rental of the particular locality, State agency claims will be made without prior approval of the 

Social Security Administration. Prior review and approval of this type of cost will be made only at the request of the State public assistance agency.

Service and Maintenance Costs in Lieu of Rent in Public Build ings.--Federal financial participation is available in the cost of service and maintenance, in lieu of rent, that can be identified as the expense of maintaining the space suitable for continuous occupancy. Expense identified as service and maintenance may include the costs of heat, light, power, insurance, elevator service, janitor service, cleaning, painting, decorating, and maintenance repairs, and in addition a reasonable amount for depreciation if such a charge can be supported. 

Depreciation in this sense is physical depreciation that can be measured within definite limits, and related to the life expectancy of the particular building. 

Service and maintenance expense may be actual charges or calculated on the basis of a uniform per square foot charge, provided the unit cost is a reasonable approximation of such

It is recognized that on occasion it has been difficult for the State agency to secure exact data on costs of service and maintenance, particularly when it has not had previous experience in claiming rental costs in public buildings. Consequently, a reasonable approximation of such expense will be considered satisfactory if it has been determined on a valid basis. This approximation of service and maintenance cost would amount to the usual commercial rental charge that has had extracted from it the part of the charge representing taxes and net profit to the owner present in a private rental transaction.

All costs of service and maintenance for State and local office space that do not exceed 75 percent of the lowest appraisal of the comparable rental may be claimed under the principles and standards in the State's approved plan providing for the approval of such costs by the State agency. 

For such claims the State agency must have available for review, upon request, evidence that the amount to be claimed is related to actual cost and has been determined to be less than 75 percent of the lowest appraisal of the comparable rental.

Rental Charges in Municipally or Federally Owned Buildings.-- 

Federal financial participation will be available in rental charges in municipal and Federal buildings on the same basis as for private rentals. When a State agency in either municipally or Federally owned office space is required to pay a rental charge for such space, participation will be available, provided the municipality is not responsible for the administration of the public assistance programs and the charge that is made does not exceed the comparable rental of the particular ocality. Although municipal and Federal authorities are not usually directly responsible for the administration of the public assistance programs, they have a local or national interest in the promotion of the programs. 

Office space facilities under such auspices would be tax free and would not ordinarily be operated to provide a profit, as would be true in a private rental arrangement. Consequently, it would be presumed that the rental charges in such situations would not be equivalent to the costs of privately owned space. 

The State agency may claim participation in such rental charges in the same manner. as for private rentals and will have available the necessary supporting data, cluding the determination of comparable rental, for review upon request. When a municipal agency is responsible for the administration of the public assistance programs, participation in the costs of office space will be governed by the aspects of policy applicable to occupancy of space in other publicly owned buildings. 

Costs of "Repairs and Alterations to Privatly or Publicly 

Owned Buildings.--Federal financial participation is available in the cost of repairs and alterations of space occupied by the State or local public assistance agency in publicly or privately owned buildings. Such costs must be necessary for the maintenance of proper facilities for the administration of the public assistance programs. Except as stated below, the 

State agency may claim such expense as administrative expenditures in full at the time the expenditures are made, providing the total amount claimed for repairs and alterations for any one building for any nual period does not exceed the cost of comparable rental. In the event the State agency is claiming other office space costs for a particular building and intends to claim the cost of repairs and alterations for the same building, such cost must be taken into account in determining that the total cost is claimed in accordance with the comparable rental.

However, expenditures for repairs and alterations made to a publicly owned building and incurred in connection with or subsequent to the initial cost of construction or purchase, in which Federal financial participation is claimed as rental charges, must be considered as a part of the original buiïding cost. The cost of such repairs and alterations may not be spread separately or claimed as a lump sum. In such event the 

State agency will need to amend its plan for spreading the cost of the building to take into account these additional costs of repairs and alterations.

The following principles will govern Federal financial participation in the costs of repairs and alterations: 1. 2. 

The benefit of the repairs and alterations will be realized by the public assistance agency within the period of occupancy of the building, commensurate with the extent of cost involved. Should the State agency vacate a building that has been repaired, and the full benefit of the expenditure has not been realized, an adjustment will be made in the State agency's expenditure claim.

The economy of the expenditures has been established by comparison with the amount of rent that would be required for other suitable space of comparable location, construction, facilities, and condition, with particular concern to the adaptability of the space repaired for the use of the public assistance agency. 

3. Where space is also occupied by agencies other than the public assistance agency, the cost of the repairs and alterations is allocated on the basis of a reasonable pro-rata share and charged to each of the agencies sharing the space.

The records in support of such expenditure will be maintained by the State agency and will be available for review upon request.

Distinction Between Maintenance Repairs and Repairs and 

Alterations.--The distinction between maintenance repairs and repairs and alterations would usually depend on whether the expenditure is for "up-keep"; that is, to keep the building in continuous running order, or whether the repair was of an 

Transmitted by State Letter No. 236 

1.

4532.

Part V. 

4000-4999 

   Administrative Expenditures 

12/23/54 extensive nature involving substantial structural changes or replacements. The cost and the anticipated life of the repair will throw light on whether or not it is of an extensive nature. 

The duration or permanency of a repair can generally be ascertained and often will define the nature of the change. For example, the patching of a roof would be a maintenance repair in contrast to a replacement of a whole roof which might have a life expectancy of 10 to 30 years depending upon the type of material used (that is, shingle, asbestos, or slate). Another factor to be considered in defining maintenance as apart from major repair is the cost of certain improvements that make structural changes in buildings. Such costs would generally involve rather substantial alterations beyond those of a maintenance character. For example, the construction or removal of partitions, or the installation of heating and plumbing systems, could not be considered as maintenance items. 

Some guidance can be achieved in defining those usual activities that are clearly of a maintenance character. To illustrate, these would involve costs of painting and decorating as well as the costs of certain preparatory work in painting and decorating, such as the washing of walls or repairing of plaster, costs of repairs to doors and locks, minor or routine repairs to elevators, plumbing and electrical equipment, and renewal of small parts as distinguished from major replacement of obsolete or worn-out equipment. You may find the following definitions, from the Accountants' Handbook,1/ helpful: "Maintenance costs may be defined so as to exclude replacement cost or original cost of distinct machine units or structure 11 has defined 

"The U.S. Treasury Department repair cost for income tax purposes as expenditures which neither materially add to the value of the property nor appreciably prolong its life, but merely keep it in an ordinary efficient operating condition." ... by an improvement is generally meant an alteration or structural change in a building or unit of equipment which results in a better piece of property, in the sense of greater durability or increased productivity or efficiency." 

Paton, W.A., editor, Accountants' Handbook, 1943, pp. 661, 659, 668.

Rental Charges Based on the Initial Cost of Construction or Purchase of Publicly Owned Buildings 

Federal financial participation is available in rental charges based on the initial costs of construction or purchase of publicly owned buildings acquired for the occupancy of the State or local public assistance agency. Federal funds are not available as lump sum amounts to defray the initial cost of construction or purchase of publicly owned buildings. In making Federal funds available for this purpose, the Federal Government obtains no equity in any building that the State may construct or purchase. 

However, by securing Federal participation through the spread of the initial costs of construction and purchase, the State agency may be able to secure more adequate space, designed for its own specific purposes and at a more reasonable cost than if the space were rented from private sources. Such participation may be claimed for the initial costs of buildings that are planned in advance of occupancy or specifically acquired for the use of the public assistance agency. Federal financial participation in the cost of buildings that may have been owned by the State and used by other agencies of the State prior to occupancy by the State or local public assistance agency will be available only on the basis of costs of service and maintenance and depreciation, if the latter can be justified. 

Ordinarily the costs of initial construction or purchase are spread over the life of the building. For purposes of Federal financial participation, however, such charges may be spread in relation to the comparable rental. The spread of costs of construction or purchase on the basis of the comparable rental permits the State agency to secure the cost of the building at a more rapid rate than if the cost were spread on the basis of the life of the building. Once the initial cost of a building has been liquidated in this manner, only the costs of service and maintenance may be charged. Depreciation may not be included as a cost of service and maintenance where Federal participation is obtained through the spread of initial costs of construction or purchase. As a result of receiving participation in defraying the initial cost of publicly owned buildings, the State agency and the Federal Government can anticipate the eventual reduction of costs that will be charged for office space. 

H.T. No. 20

4532.5-p.2

4532.5 Rental Charges Based on the Initial Cost of Construction or Purchase of Publicly Owned Buildings (Continued) 

If the State agency should vacate a building before the end of the life expectancy period, an adjustment of the Federal funds that the agency received will be made if the spread of the cost has been over a shorter period in accordance with the comparable rental. 

The amount of funds that the State agency received on the comparable rental basis, as compared to the amount of funds that it would have received had the initial cost of the building actually been spread in accordance with the life of the building, would need to be determined and an adjustment in the claim for Federal participation made (see V-4532.51). Consequently, the life expectancy of each building must be established prior to a request for participation in order to determine the period of time in which the State agency would receive the full benefit of the expenditure.

Under certain circumstances Federal financial participation is available in other expenses as a part of the cost of a project for the construction or purchase of a publicly owned building. 

These expenses might include the interest on monies secured from private or public sources to be used in the financing of the building; the cost of plans, specifications, and architects' fees; and repairs and alterations necessary to make the building suitable to the purposes of the State agency.

The costs of landscaping beyond the immediate surroundings of the building are not considered a proper cost of the building. The construction of sidewalks and the seeding and sodding of bare areas would entail proper costs. Such costs are appropriate, for purposes of Federal financial participation, only to the extent that they are necessary to the use of the particular building. 

If there are subsequent requests for participation in any additional office space expense, after the execution of a particular plan for spreading the initial costs of construction or purchase, it will be necessary that the plan for spreading the initial cost be amended to take into account these additional costs. 

All rental charges for the initial cost of construction or purchase of publicly owned buildings for occupancy by State or local public assistance agencies, not exceeding 75 percent of the lowest appraisal of the comparable rental, may be approved by the State agency under the standards in its approved plan providing for such '6X
